dropped its wake: nothing re-ran it until another settlement of the project or
a restart. The failure rate grows with the number of committing Workers and the
snapshot size (the read window). The fault identity named the task's checkout
path and error class without an episode, so a failure matching an earlier, even
resolved, signal recorded nothing.

Decision:
- Recovery never decides from discovery. Recipient settlement walks the compact
  assignment order index and materializes only its recipient, project-wide
  continuation candidates, and older rows without immutable recovery metadata.
  The metadata commits with each grant; no backfill or read-time write is needed.
  Boot and dependency walks still discover every assignment. There is no snapshot
  witness: index rows and documents commit together and sequences never change.
  Settled request ids come from the settlement order index. Every decision still comes from
  the request's own point snapshot, the authorization CAS or live admission, so
  concurrent writers cannot make discovery uncertain. `recover_project_dispatch`
  and `recover_coordinator` share one discovery per pass.
- A pass that cannot complete is a durable pending wake
  (`lib/project_dispatch/pending_wakes.py`; contract `wakeNamespace`,
  `$defs.pendingWakes`): `source:` one request's Coordinator wake, `recipient:`
  a drain that failed before it reached its requests, `dependents:` one
  prerequisite's dependents, `project` a timed or commitment wake. An entry is
  processed until a pass for it completes, by any later recovery pass of the
  project, the project's next dispatch commit in this process that concerns the
  entry (the writer whose commit made a read lose is that next storage event; a
  commit during a pass runs one more pass of the entries it concerns after it;
  see "A commit runs only the entries whose pass reads a document it wrote"
  below), the task-maintenance tick and startup.
  Completion removes it under the record CAS. Exactly once remains the wake's
  command identity and authorization CAS; a CAS snapshot that derives another
  wake raises the transient `WakeEvidenceChanged`, which keeps the entry.
- An entry is one fault episode: its first failure records one typed Watchdog
  fault (`coordinator_wake_failed`, or `recovery_failed` for dependents) whose
  identity names the entry and its `since`; later failures of the episode add
  none, and completion resolves it. A failed drain keeps its single report call
  site in `turn_lifecycle._drain_queue_after_settlement`, one occurrence per
  failed drain; its `recipient:` entry adds no second report. A refused birth
  (lane busy, human queued, prerequisites not ready) is logged with its reason;
  the authorized row stays and the lane's own settlement starts it.

No retry count, interval or timeout was added: a commit, the existing
maintenance tick and startup are the events, and `limits.casAttempts` still
bounds each CAS.

Alternatives rejected:
- retrying the witnessed full read: its window loses, not its attempt count; a
  quiet window of seconds never comes while Workers commit;
- a consistent multi-document storage read: a new SQL and native operation
  bounded by RPC pages, for a read that only discovers;
- an unwitnessed per-request proposal read: a torn request could publish a
  stalled-request report before the CAS rechecks it;
- a recipient index: a backfill migration of every existing grant.

Remaining: the per-request snapshot and the authorization CAS are still fenced
by the head (a wake's frontier summarizes the request's rows, and a new row
takes a head sequence). Under sustained writes they can lose; the entry stays
and the next commit runs it again. "One dispatch head per owner and project"
below is the structural relief.

A commit runs only the entries whose pass reads a document it wrote
(2026-11-12). Problem: on 2026-11-12 every project's record held `source:`
entries that had failed for up to 7.6 hours, all with `WakeEvidenceChanged`;
each of the execution host's 2.46 `project.dispatch.commit`/s (almost all
Worker occurrences of other requests, document commits) re-ran a full
recovery pass over all of them, 11.2% of the host's GIL samples in a 45 s
`py-spy --gil` profile. A commit that rewrote none of an entry's documents
cannot change that pass's outcome. Decision: a document commit names the
entries it concerns (`pending_wakes.written_entries`: an assignment row's
`source:`, `recipient:` for its recipient and Coordinator, `dependents:` for
itself and its prerequisites; a settlement's and a request budget's
`source:`); `notify_commit` starts a pass of only those entries, and a commit
that concerns no pending entry costs a set intersection. A head commit
(creation or walk-fenced update, which can tear any entry's walk or change
discovery) or a legacy record commit concerns every entry, as do the
maintenance tick, startup and recovery passes. Head commits are rare: both busy
projects' heads advanced 0 times in a sampled minute while Workers committed.
Spec: `test_a_wake_failing_on_unchanged_evidence_runs_no_pass_per_unrelated_commit`
(20 unrelated Worker commits: 20 passes before, 0 after; one head commit: 1).
The stuck entries' cause was the admission CAS's narrow read (above, "A
Coordinator wake's grant CAS also selects the source cohort", fde171643a,
landed after the execution host's 21:13 start): replaying both derivations
read-only against the 15 live entries, the old CAS selection derived a
different wake for all 15 and the fixed one the identical wake. An entry for a
request that no longer warrants a wake (settled, no proposal, an authority
verdict) completes in that pass. Alternatives rejected: a retry interval,
backoff or cap per entry (a tuning knob that still re-runs unchanged
failures); a fingerprint of the evidence the failing pass read (it spans Turn,
attempt, binding and board reads, and computing it is most of a pass).

Specifications: `tests/storage/test_project_dispatch_wake_contention.py`
(SQLite and native: a drain completes while a writer commits during every
document read; pending entries per episode; commit relevance) and
`tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_wake_durability.py` (a contended wake
delivered once by the next commit, concurrent passes, a failed drain, the
commitment-due wake, a new episode after completion). Measurement:
`benchmarks/wake_drain_contention.py`.

### Decisions with a default

The Coordinator no longer receives this tool (see "Session-free coordination":
it asks with `ask_human(blocking=false)` and proceeds on its stated default).
The mechanism remains for decisions already recorded.
`request_user_decision(report_turn_id, recommendation, wait_seconds=1800)`
followed a `report_to_user(kind=decision)`. It records `pendingDecision` on the
request account and keeps the request open; independent Workers continue. If a
human Turn arrives after the report, that new request steers the work and the
default is not applied. Otherwise, at `dueAt` recovery births a continuation
with `coordinatorWake.reason = decision_default` whose brief says to apply the
recommendation and say so in the next report, and marks the decision
`default_applied` in the same CAS. `finish_project_request(outcome=needs_user)`
remains for cases without a sensible default.

Problem: the continuation brief still told every continuation to follow a
decision report with `request_user_decision`, which session-free coordination
removed from the Coordinator ceiling, so a continuation that needed the human
was refused with advice to delegate. Decision: the brief names only tools its
grant froze. When `admission.asks_human` admits the continuation profile, the
brief says to ask with `ask_human(blocking=false)` and state the default applied
meanwhile. An older grant without `ask_human` proceeds on its recommended default
and says so in its next report, or, without a sensible default, reports
`kind=decision` and finishes with `needs_user`. Alternatives rejected: returning
`request_user_decision` to the ceiling (restores the report-then-wait flow that
session-free coordination removed) and always naming `ask_human` (older grants
cannot call it). Spec: `test_continuation_brief_names_only_tools_its_grant_holds`.

Timed wakes (backoff and due decisions) are a process-local `threading.Timer`,
one per owner/project keeping the earliest due time. They only call the same
idempotent recovery pass; the due time itself is derived from durable records,
so a lost timer is recovered by startup or any later settlement. Rejected: a
durable scheduler job per request (a second authority over the same state) and
indefinite waiting for the human (contradicts unattended completion).

Code delivery is automatic: the Coordinator's review with a checkpoint authorizes
publication, the delivery receiver merges the task delta into the canonical
checkout and, in a repository that opted into automatic commits, commits exactly
that delta onto the current branch, with no human step
([project integration](modules/project_integration.md#task-owned-canonical-delivery),
"Reviewed project deliveries become commits"). A delta that built on uncommitted
canonical bytes stays delivered as working-tree bytes; its `entangled`
acknowledgement names the paths.

Alternatives rejected: treating a notification as human authority, adding a
second scheduler/task state, and reusing a generic queued message with mutable
capabilities. Verification: concurrent notification and finish/delegate races,
lane priority, authority withdrawal, stall backoff, decision defaults, unknown
consumption and native storage restart in `test_coordinator_continuation.py` and
`test_coordinator_workers_authority.py`. Native restart runs one continuation
through ordinary tool assembly/model accounting/review, then actual Git delivery,
report unread state and durable completion; repeated recovery sends nothing.
Internal continuation rows produce neither Worker nodes nor human review cards.
Measured in the deterministic two-assignment fixture: 50 idle callbacks took
0.539 seconds, retained 5,470 bytes unchanged, and produced zero new births,
durable writes or threads. This is metadata-path evidence, not a provider latency
claim. The native journey checks real physical accounting separately.

### Conversation-first project entry

Problem: a small top-strip workspace button hid the bound Coordinator among date
buckets and made the management drawer feel like the primary work surface.
Decision: `project-coordination-surface.ts` owns a fixed sidebar card outside
Today/Yesterday groups. Its scope follows the selected folder (an unconfigured
folder never inherits another conversation's project), while the center follows
the active conversation. Clicking the sidebar entry opens the bound Coordinator
in the ordinary chat canvas directly, never a management dialog, including when
that conversation is outside the loaded catalog page:
`project-conversation-navigation.ts` resolves server metadata through the catalog
owner, then existing TurnStore hydration owns the transcript. Later navigation
or a new draft fences late reads.

An unbound card offers **Create project Coordinator** explicitly; it calls the
create/bind facade only on click, disables duplicate clicks, shows errors
for retry and requires a selected project folder. Opening an ordinary chat never
promotes it. Historical same-project chats cannot be selected as Coordinator.

Problem: that folder requirement was evaluated only when the card was painted.
When the open conversation already showed the project without a selected
folder (for example after a reload restored it), Create painted disabled with
the select-folder hint, and selecting that project's folder changed neither the
read key (project, conversation, sidebar path) nor the painted facts, so the
button stayed disabled until another conversation was opened. Decision: folder
selection is local state, so every surface refresh, which each conversation-list
render (including a folder selection) triggers, re-evaluates the painted Create
control and its hint from `canCreate` without a read. Alternatives rejected:
adding the selection to the read key re-reads binding, board, assignments and
reports for a local change and keeps the stale button until they return;
waiting or re-clicking in the browser journey hides the defect. Specs:
`project-coordination-surface.test.ts` and
`tests/frontend/test_project_coordinator_browser.py`.

Problem: a folder click cleared the sidebar container and painted the card only
after the binding read resolved, so the conversation list jumped up and back
down while the entry popped in late. Decision: every project/folder switch paints
the card shell synchronously (`aria-busy`, localized loading line) before the
read starts, and the surface retains the last resolved Coordinator per project so
a return visit paints the real identity — and the list filters that conversation
— on the first frame. A failed read converts the pre-painted shell into the
error state instead of collapsing the container. A taller skeleton, a fixed
spacer, or hiding the entry outside a bound Coordinator were rejected: the first
two still shift the list, and the third removes the only route into an unbound
project.

Problem: the canvas (`#presenceStrip`, above the transcript) is cleared on every
conversation switch and repainted only after all its project reads settle,
up to seconds on the live server (2026-11-02 owner report). Moving between
chats of a project with a Coordinator, its "Return to Coordinator" row
vanished, the transcript grew into its space, and the row then pushed the
reading down again. Decision: the canvas surface keeps the newest read set
whose binding resolved, per mounted project. Binding, task board and reports
are project facts, so a same-project switch repaints the
new chat's canvas from that set synchronously: a related chat keeps a usable
route and the strip its geometry, while an unrelated chat drops the route at
once. The same fresh reads still run, and their paint replaces the canvas only
when the facts differ. The repaint does not refresh the conversation list,
which it cannot change. The task board belongs to the binding, so opening the
Coordinator paints it at once from that set too; it never follows into another
chat. A project switch or teardown discards the
set. This is a first paint, not a read cache, so the rejection of caching
results across push frames still holds. Alternatives rejected: keeping the old
row until the reads land shows a route that may not apply and whose handler is
fenced to the previous chat; reserving a fixed strip height leaves blank space
where no route applies. Specs: `project-coordination-surface.test.ts` and
`tests/visual/test_visual_conversation_open_stability.py`, which keeps a
followed transcript pinned when the strip appears.

Problem: the loading shell waited for binding, board, assignments and reports
as one batch; a stalled optional read blocked navigation, retries and later
folder selections. Decision: sidebar navigation becomes usable once the server
binding resolves, and the same renderer enriches its metadata after the other
reads settle. A binding failure exposes Retry immediately and cancels sibling
reads. Each surface owns an AbortController: scope changes and teardown retire
old reads without queuing the new project behind them; late responses cannot
replace the current view. The endpoint facade gives these read-only requests a
12-second transport deadline, including body consumption, and preserves existing
failure states. Successful refreshes retain the row while metadata is pending.
Alternatives rejected: waiting for every optional read repeats the blockage;
using a cached identity as authority bypasses the binding check; polling or a
second timeout wrapper duplicates existing invalidation and transport owners.
Specs: `project-coordination-surface.test.ts` and
`api/endpoint-registry/project-coordination-read.test.ts`.

Problem: a failed binding read left a permanent sidebar warning because an
unchanged list-render signature suppresses reads and the project subscription
missed reconnects. A later successful binding also left the warning visible
while optional board/report reads were pending. Decision: failed surfaces retry
on browser online/visible recovery, and the existing project subscription also
revalidates after push reconnect. The application's shared revalidation
(`_revalidateOnResume`: backend-availability recovery and its periodic
reconcile, besides those signals) calls each surface's `recover()` through
`runtimeScope.recoverProjectCoordination`, so a binding read that timed out
while the API host stalled is retried without a click and without a surface
timer. Recovery uses the same single flight and scope fencing; a read already
in flight is the retry, so one signal heard on both paths reads once. Healthy
signals and ordinary list renders start no reads. A successful binding immediately replaces the failed row, independent
of optional data. Teardown removes both browser and reconnect listeners.
Failure copy distinguishes transport timeout/network errors and logs only the
canonical code/status/request ID, not response bodies. Alternatives rejected:
raising deadlines cannot recover already-settled failures; retrying every list
render creates a self-triggering failure loop; cached binding identity cannot
replace server authority. Specs include reconnect and browser recovery journeys
in `project-coordination-entry.test.ts`, `project-coordination-surface.test.ts`
and `tests/frontend/test_project_coordinator_loading.py`.

The isolated full-app journey additionally covers an initial binding failure,
explicit Retry and recovery, selection through live catalog updates, and the
double-encoded proxy stream URL. It uses a seeded project, Coordinator and
Workers on the isolated live-server fixture; it does not infer these behaviors
from a unit-level surface mock. Spec:
`tests/frontend/test_coordinator_live_ui_recovery.py`.

Problem: under heavy local load, background binding re-reads (project push
signals, conversation switches) intermittently hit the 12-second deadline or
lost their connection, and each failure stacked "Project workspace unavailable
· timeout" plus Retry under a card that was still usable: the binding had not
changed, and the same read answered in well under a second once load fell.
Decision: after a mount paints a binding from a successful read, a re-read
failure that an identical repeat can outlive (`isTransientApiFailure` in
`api/errors.ts`: transport timeout/network, 408/429/502/503/504, an
unattributed 500, retryable storage codes) keeps that binding usable and
unflagged. It is stale demand: online/visible recovery, push reconnect and the
next project signal re-read it, without flashing the card back to loading. The
canonical code/status/request ID is still logged. First reads, folder-switch
shells (whose cached identity is never authority) and definitive answers still
show the failure with Retry. Alternatives rejected: a longer deadline only moves
the threshold under load; a retry timer violates the no-polling rule; hiding
every failure would also hide a definitive rejection; a new local list of
transient statuses would duplicate the classifier send startup already owned, so
that classifier moved to the API error owner. Specs:
`project-coordination-surface.test.ts`, `api/errors.test.ts` and
`tests/frontend/test_project_coordinator_loading.py`.

Problem: the canvas and the sidebar entry are separate surfaces, and each
subscribes to `project` pushes. push.js calls both handlers synchronously for
one WebSocket frame, so every frame issued the binding, board, assignments and
reports reads twice. On 2026-11-01 the live server logged 511 assignment-page
GETs, peaking at 24 per minute. The client abandoned 25% of them, and each page
build cost seconds. Decision (supersedes the earlier rejection of canvas and
sidebar coalescing): the presence adapter routes the read ports of both surfaces
(binding, board, assignments, reports, budget and roster) through one
`createSameTaskReadJoin` from `project-coordination-read-join.ts`. Identical
reads issued in the same task share one request. The key is the endpoint plus
every argument except the signal: path, cursor, `commandId` and
`assignmentId`. A microtask retires each entry, so a frame in a later task
always reads again. The earlier rejection held that a joined pre-push request
could leave one surface stale. A same-task join cannot attach to a read issued
before a later frame, because each frame is its own task. Each caller keeps its
own AbortSignal. Aborting rejects only that caller. The shared request aborts
only when its last caller aborts, and a request that every caller has left is
never joined again. Joined callers receive one result object, which consumers
treat as immutable; the specs serve frozen results.

Each surface still keeps one read in flight plus one trailing refresh.
Trailing refreshes join only when both start in one task. With a Coordinator
chat open, the canvas runs its roster and plan reads after the shared reads, so
its trailing refresh starts later than the sidebar's and the two do not join.
The shipped push.js, presence adapter and surfaces were measured with scripted
latencies: 3 s for the assignment page and 0.3 s for each other read. With idle
frames on a Coordinator chat, a frame issued 7 GETs instead of 11, and 1
assignment GET instead of 2. On an ordinary project chat it issued 5 instead of
9. At 24 frames per minute, an ordinary chat issued 0.84 assignment GETs per
frame instead of 1.68. A Coordinator chat issued 1.60 instead of 1.61.

Alternatives rejected: the transport `coalesce` option shares a GET until it
settles. It needs signal-free requests and lets a read issued after a push
receive the response to a request issued before it. A debounce or time window
adds a tuning interval and delays every paint. One subscription for both
surfaces would couple the folder-scoped sidebar to the conversation-scoped
canvas and to its abort lifecycle. Caching results across frames would hide the
change a frame announces. Joining any in-flight read issued after the latest
frame would also cover trailing refreshes, but every local invalidation would
then have to end that sharing first: mutations, Retry, recovery and reconnect.
Specs: `project-coordination-entry.test.ts` drives the shipped push.js, presence
adapter and surfaces.

The bound chat shows a compact work list, eight rows per visible page. The
recorded dependency graph is collapsed and mounted only on disclosure, preserving
space for conversation and composer. The user's fold state survives live
refreshes. The demand-loaded management dialog opens only explicitly, from the
sidebar row's 负责人设置 ("Coordinator settings") of a project whose
Coordinator is unbound or unavailable; it is not the primary navigation action. The task board's footer
offers 历史任务 instead ([Task history](#task-history-owner-request-2026-11-11)).

Problem: repeated completion/failure labels and status lanes buried the actual
work and did not show who delegated it or which result another task needs.
Decision: `project-coordination-work-list.ts` renders actionable, bounded work
rows first; `project-coordination-canvas.ts` exposes the optional graph as
selectable Coordinator/Worker nodes. Dashed delegation, solid dependency and dotted rework
edges describe only recorded relationships. The Coordinator remains the root
while its conversation is open. Historical Board records without assignment
links remain unconnected; the UI never guesses relationships from titles or order.

Current work and its prerequisites are visible first. Unrelated completed/cancelled
history is an explicit disclosure, and the selected node stays visible when it
finishes. Selection reveals result, dependency, revocation and delivery evidence
and an action opening the ordinary Worker conversation. Counts and coverage are
secondary disclosed information. Project-local selection, history and bounded
zoom survive push refreshes; a project change clears them. Container queries
select matching one/two/three-column card and SVG geometry without observers,
new polling or model calls. Native scrolling preserves readable node sizes on
narrow screens. All controls use the shared kit; EN/ZH labels, keyboard access
and three themes share the same view. The fixed sidebar entry remains navigation.
Long titles occupy at most three lines and result digests one line inside each
fixed-size node; the selection inspector preserves the full title and detail.
Its conversation action wraps within narrow panes. Expanding node height was
rejected because it breaks the shared node/edge geometry; truncating stored
content would discard evidence. Browser coverage includes long localized text
inside the ordinary conversation summary, not only an isolated graph.
Alternatives rejected: a graph-first display makes routine status expensive to
scan; eliminating the graph loses dependency evidence. An LLM-generated chart
adds cost and can invent state; another conversation renderer duplicates Turn
ownership. SVG lines and ordinary DOM controls preserve accessible actions.
Ordinary chats have no Worker role label and no top workspace shortcut; actual
related-work evidence alone can offer a return-to-Coordinator link. The card is
not duplicated in date groups. Alternatives rejected: automatic current-chat
binding, a second chat renderer, and a new polling loop. Project push invalidation
coalesces reads; stable facts retain DOM/focus and expanded state. These are source
contracts, not claims that a running service has loaded this checkout.

### Lead office (owner decision 2026-11-12)

Status (owner decision 2026-11-17, "经典模式和普通对话一样，动画只留在 Studio"): the
lead office is not mounted anywhere. The classic sidebar shows each lead as plain
rows: its entry row and the archive of the conversations it started (its
deliverables read on its task board, "Lead deliverables"), with each Worker's
status and the running tally. The studio (`#/world`, `features/world/`) is the only
animated surface; it hides the classic sidebar (`01-workspace-dock.css`). The
2026-11-15 rule below gated the sidebar office on the stored landing choice
(`tofu_landing_workspace`), which is `world` by default and after every studio visit, so the
office appeared in the classic chat view, the one place that sidebar is seen; that
mode read and its change subscription are removed with it.
Problem: the owner saw animated office rooms per Worker under 项目负责人 in the classic
view. Decision: remove the mount from the common owner (`presence.js`) rather than
re-key its gate on the shown workspace, because the sidebar is never visible in the
studio, so no gate could ever show it there. Alternatives considered: gating on
`body[data-workspace]` (a dead branch: the studio hides the sidebar); hiding the slot
with CSS (would still load and run the scene). The `features/lead-office/` module,
its stylesheet and copy remain in the tree, unreferenced by the runtime, until the
studio redesign decides whether any of it is reused; the text below records its
design. Specifications: `project-coordination-entry.test.ts` (plain rows whatever the
stored landing) and `tests/frontend/test_lead_sidebar_plain_browser.py` (real
browser, both stored landings, studio round trip).

Problem: owners prefer talking to their leads, and the multi-level lead system
already coordinates the work, so the Goal Studio's game office had become a
separate destination owners do not open: a game reachable only through its own
entry is never discovered. A first version put the office in a band above each
lead chat; the owner rejected it the same day. The band duplicated the sidebar's
list of conversations the lead started, which the office was meant to replace,
and it pushed the transcript away from its composer, so the input box felt
detached from the conversation.

Decision: the office is the team list. Beneath a lead's sidebar entry, where the
conversations it started used to open, the lead's team is a tower: the lead's
own office on top, then one floor per team member. A project folder shows its
coordinator's floor, with one floor per Worker desk in delegation order. A desk
exists while its assignment needs the lead (queued, blocked, working, a result
awaiting review, an unreviewed failure); reviewed, cancelled, revoked and
evidence-less history leaves. A worker chat the lead started that the catalog shows
running without an assignment on the page (a `create_conversation` chat, or one
older than the page) is a working desk too, so the office never shows less than
the sidebar's running count. The uncategorized view, where the main coordinator's
card stands, shows the headquarters: its office, one floor per project with that
project's coordinator, then its own Worker desks; the project rail stays the way to
move between floors. The Router Watchdog has no task board and no office.

The conversation keeps the main view, its composer right under its transcript;
nothing is inserted between them. Every room opens its conversation there: the
lead's office its chat, a desk its worker chat, a project floor that project's
folder and coordinator. The open conversation's room is framed and marked
`aria-current`, as a list marks its selected row, and the office stays put while
the owner moves between the lead and its workers. The entry's row stays the
office's title bar (role, headline, unread report, ID); the conversations it
started remain reachable in the archive group right beneath the office, without a
running tally of its own while the office shows that work. A tall team scrolls
inside the office's slot, as the archive does, so the owner's own chats keep their
share of the sidebar. The stage is tap-only (no wheel, drag, pinch or movement
keys), so the sidebar scrolls through it; the canvas is sized to the tower.

Everything shown is projected (`features/lead-office/projection.ts`) from
confirmed facts, never manufactured: the assignment page that Manage work reads;
the binding, board and report the coordination surface reads for the shown entry
(the sidebar surface on a floor, a router-home surface at the headquarters); live
run flags of the loaded catalog; and at the headquarters `GET
/api/v4/router/projects` (the `get_router_projects` facts, docs/ROUTER.md). The
lead works at its desk while replying, comes to the front door while it waits for
the owner's answer and stands at the task board otherwise; a working desk sits at
a lit screen; a result awaiting review stands beside its desk with a result sheet
and a failure with an alert; an unread report lies on the delivery table and the
board shows its tally. Each floor names its title and state on its bare left wall.
Reads are event-driven (showing a lead, a project push, a desk's or the lead's
live state changing), one flight at a time, with no polling; the scene sleeps
between keyframes. The office reuses the office game engine (`features/office-game/engine/stage.ts`:
loop, camera, input, hidden/offscreen sleep, accessible targets), loaded when a
lead first shows, with its own deferred stylesheet; its art is its own (below).
A desk is named by its Worker conversation's own catalog title; until the catalog
has one, by its brief's first line with a lead's dated request label (`Owner
request (2026-11-15): "…"`) and quotes removed (`briefTitle`).

Alternatives considered: a band above the lead chat (the first version) duplicates
the sidebar list and separates the composer from the transcript; a separate game
screen entered from the lead card is a destination owners do not find; making the
game the main view with the conversation in a dialogue box repeats the studio
owners moved away from; one studio copy for both leads repeats one surface; an
office for project leads only omits the main coordinator; a new progress endpoint
or polling loop would duplicate the assignment, board and report authorities;
plain list rows lose the spatial, at-a-glance reading the art gives.

Until 2026-11-17 the office was the studio mode's sidebar only (owner decision
2026-11-15), keyed on the stored landing choice; that coupling is removed (status
above). Specifications of the unmounted module: `frontend/src/features/lead-office/*.test.ts`.

### Lead office art and state mapping (owner request 2026-11-15)

Problem: the first office cropped bands out of the Goal Studio's 960-unit
raster rooms and scaled them to a ~260 px sidebar: rooms became dark strips, a
gradient over the left half carried a truncated raw brief ("Owner request
(2026-11-15): …"), characters were ~10 px sprites, and nothing showed a hand-off,
a review or a delivery.

Decision: one coherent art direction drawn for the sidebar's scale, procedural
vector art, no raster assets (`features/lead-office/art.ts`, geometry in
`layout.ts`, behavior in `scene.ts`).

- **View**: a side-view cutaway tower, 320 units wide (about one CSS px in a
  sidebar). The lead's penthouse (184 u) on top, one 96 u floor per teammate;
  every room is a cross-section (slab, back wall, wainscot, plank floor) with the
  elevator shaft as the tower's spine on the right; people arrive through its
  door and hand-offs travel down it.
- **Style and light**: flat-shaded shapes in one warm palette, contact shadows,
  ambient occlusion at wall/floor and ceiling, a pendant lamp pool per room. Key
  light comes from the windows, and daylight follows the viewer's local clock:
  sky gradient, sun or moon, drifting clouds by day, the city's lit windows at
  night, the window beam on the floor, night dimming and warmer lamps. Signals
  are reserved: amber asks, red fails, gold delivers, green is done, blue waits.
- **People**: 2.6-head chibi figures on a jointed rig (legs with knee, capsule
  torso, bent arms, head with hair style and role mark: tie, headphones,
  glasses, beret, visor, cap, scarf), drawn per pose rather than sampled from
  sheets, so sitting, typing and walking interpolate. A person's look is its
  role silhouette (`modelForRole`, shared with the studio) varied by identity.
- **Text**: room names sit on pinned paper cards (state stripe, two title lines,
  state with its dot) drawn in screen space for crispness; hover or keyboard
  focus shows a status card with the full title, state sentence and "open".

Asset inventory (all in `art.ts`, painted into offscreen layers once per device
scale): the lead's penthouse (panelled walls, corkboard frame, panorama frame,
rug, plant, clock, bookshelf, side table with outbox tray, two pendants); one
floor per department (`engineer`/`executor` server rack and `{ }` print,
`researcher` books and globe, `reviewer` filing cabinet and checklist,
`writer` books, papers and plant, `analyst` chart monitor and line chart,
`general`/`coordinator` books, plant and landscape print; tints per
department); the floor desk, the lead's two-monitor desk, the office chair, the
shaft and its door frame. Live per-frame painters: windows and skyline, door
panels, screens (`off`, `code`, `paused`, `done`, `error`), people, bubbles
(`typing`, `ask`, `think`, `wait` hourglass, `alert`), the brief folder, result
sheet, gold report folder, envelope, confetti and sparkle, room lighting.

State mapping, one pose per projected state (`deskRest`, `leadRest`,
`projectRest`):

| Projected state | Person | Screen / props | Mark |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| desk `working` | seated, typing | code scrolling on the beat; brief on desk | — |
| desk `queued` | standing at the shaft door, holding the brief, foot tap | off | hourglass |
| desk `blocked` | seated, leaning back, hand at chin | paused (amber) | thought dots |
| desk `review` | standing beside the desk, facing out, presenting a sheet | done (green tick); sheet on desk | — |
| desk `failed` | seated, slumped on the desk | error (red) | red `!` |
| lead `replying` | seated at its desk, typing | both screens code | typing dots |
| lead `asking` | standing forward, hand raised | off | amber `?` |
| lead `idle` | standing at the task board, hand at chin | off | — |
| lead board | — | one note per task (ticked when finished) when they fit, exact progress bar and `done/total` | — |
| lead unread report | — | gold folder on the outbox tray | — |
| project floor | its coordinator: asking (questions), typing (active Workers), idle otherwise; no coordinator: dark empty room | — | as above |

Moments replay only real changes seen while the office is shown, never on first
sight or under reduced motion: a new desk (a delegation) sends a sealed brief down
the shaft from the penthouse to its floor, the door opens and the Worker walks
out carrying it, puts it on the desk and sits; a desk turning `review` makes its
Worker stand, cheer with confetti and walk to present the result; a lead report
newly unread lands on the tray with a sparkle; a lead or Worker whose place
changes walks there (`stand`, `walk`, `sit` beats). Clicking a room opens its
conversation (a project floor its project).

Performance: the stage's single `requestAnimationFrame` loop renders only when
asked. The scene asks for every frame only while someone walks, a door moves or
a burst plays; looping poses (typing, thinking dots, hourglass, raised hand)
wake it on the next 125 ms art beat; otherwise it sleeps until the next local
minute (the sky). Hidden tabs and offscreen offices own no frame or timer
(stage IntersectionObserver and `visibilitychange`). Static art is painted once
per device scale into offscreen layers bounded by room kinds; rooms, cards,
targets and text layout are prepared per view, so a steady frame allocates
nothing beyond canvas calls, and the stage skips its target signature when a
scene returns the same targets array under the same mapping. The office has no
network assets: its code is the lazy `lead-office/office` chunk, imported only
when a lead first shows (never in the classic mode).

Alternatives considered: higher-resolution pixel art needs a sprite pipeline and
per-pose sheets, and scales poorly between a 260 px sidebar and a 2x display;
keeping the studio's raster rooms keeps the cropping problem; an isometric view
needs depth sorting and diagonal hit-testing for rooms that are list rows; a
continuous 60 Hz loop for ambient motion spends CPU in an always-visible sidebar.
Specifications: `frontend/src/features/lead-office/{layout,projection,scene,office}.test.ts`
and `tests/frontend/test_lead_office_scene_browser.py` (real Canvas2D: every
state, hand-off and review captures, day and night, hover card and click, a
six-Worker profile, hidden/offscreen/disposed sleep, reduced motion; evidence
in `output/studio-redesign/after/`).

### Lead deliverables (owner request 2026-11-16)

Problem: in the classic view a lead's deliverables (reports, published
files and decks, merged Worker results) were scattered through very long
conversations, so the owner scrolled back to find them; and in the studio,
clicking a deliverable only opened the lead's conversation.

Decision: a read-time projection over the owners that already hold the
deliverables, with no store of its own (owner direction 2026-11-16: reuse the
existing report/delivery owners, no parallel store)
(`lib/conversations/lead_deliverables.py`; `deliverableItem`/`deliverablePage`
in `contracts/project_coordination_v1.schema.json`). Each read of
`GET /api/v4/project/coordination/deliverables` projects the lead's main-lane
Turns (through the per-Turn facts below) and the conversation's artifact rows:

- reports of every kind (owner request 2026-11-17; before, deliveries only):
  the lead's own canonical `coordinator_report.*` Turns (`CoordinatorReports`);
  the item's `reportKind` is the Turn's, its title and summary the Turn's
  `title\n\nmessage` content, and its `files` the `reportFile`s the publishing
  `report_to_user` call's receipt names (found in the reply that made the call,
  segment result or round `toolContent`). The Global Router's relayed reports
  are the report relay notices in its Turns: `lib/router/relay.py` owns both
  the notice format (`render_notice`, whose `Files:` line carries the attached
  files) and its parser (`notice_references`); each named report is read from
  the Project Router's canonical Turn, so a forged or deleted reference lists
  nothing.
- published files, decks and documents: the artifact rows
  (`lib.artifacts.core.list_artifacts`) from `publish_response_file`, rich
  responses and long-form reports. A `.pptx`/`.pptd` file is a deck, another
  file a file, a rich response or long-form report a document; workspace
  writes, inline images and scanner snippets are not deliverables. The source
  message is the oldest Turn whose tool round carries the `artifactId`. A file a
  report attached is listed under that report, never again as its own item.

A report Turn the lead auto-translated also carries `translation` (its
`translatedContent` split as `title\n\nmessage`), and the reports head's
`latest` carries `translatedTitle`: the list row, the report dialog and the
sidebar's unread preview show the copy the conversation shows by default, the
row tooltip keeps the original (owner request 2026-11-17: translate the
sidebar's Deliverables and Progress sections). Every label of those sections
and of the task board comes from the i18n catalogs, repaints on
`tofu:language-change`, and `scripts/check_i18n_usage.mjs` (`literalCopyFiles`)
rejects a hard-coded lettered literal in their copy positions.

Items are newest first, 64 per page (the coordination page bound); the cursor
is the previous page's last `(createdAt, item digest)`, so a page boundary is
stable while new deliverables arrive. Reads require the caller to own the
conversation (403 otherwise); a malformed cursor is a 400. Producers send only
a push hint, `{kind: "lead_deliverable", conversationId}` on the project
channel: `CoordinatorReports` for a delivery, the relay for the Global Router,
and `rich_response` for a published artifact. A Worker result a finished request
merged reaches the list through the delivery report the finish requires; the
studio's trophy shelf opens such a result as a `merge` item it builds itself.

Cost (2026-11-18, owner: "switching projects, the lead's board and each
task's reports and files take long to load"). Problem: every read walked the
whole main lane, one bounded `turn.sync.page` per Turn, and scanned every
string of every projection. The tofu lead (158 Turns, 49 MB of projections)
answered in 1-4 s on a quiet host and 8-18 s under load, once for the board's
summary on each project switch and again for each expanded task; the scan also
held the GIL inside the API process. Decision: the projection reads per-Turn
facts (`lead_deliverables.turn_facts`: the report a Turn is, its
`report_to_user` receipts, its `artifactId`s, its relay notice references),
kept by `lib/conversations/lead_turn_facts.py`. The first read of a
conversation in the API process walks the lane as before; every later read
sends the projection revision of each Turn it holds to `turn.list_delta`
(`turn_lifecycle.turns_changed_since`), which returns only the Turns updated
since the previous read's watermark whose revision advanced, plus the Turns
deleted since (tombstones), so a read costs one indexed storage read plus the
changed Turns. Invalidation is the authority's own (projection revision,
updated-at watermark with its overlap margin, tombstones): an entry is rebuilt
by the walk when the delta is refused at the authority's row or response bound
and when it was last validated longer ago than both authorities keep
tombstones (seven days, `TURN_TOMBSTONE_RETENTION_SECONDS`). Concurrent reads
of one conversation share one advance. Entries are reconstructible and dropped
by memory-pressure relief (`lib.ttl_cache`); their size is a few hundred bytes
per Turn (identity, revision, the report's title/message, receipt and
artifact ids), never a projection. Artifact rows, task history and report
links are still read fresh on each read; each is one storage read.
Alternatives rejected: replaying `turn.sync.changes` after the last read
(the change feed carries every streamed attempt event, about 60 MB in 15
minutes of the active tofu lead, more than the walk); caching the whole page
by the conversation's sync head (it moves with every streamed event, so an
active lead never hits); multi-Turn walk pages (a page size or byte budget
nothing here defines, the reason the walk reads one Turn per page); a
derived deliverables store (rejected above).

One item model and one opener serve both modes
(`frontend/src/features/lead-deliverables/`). Opening an item shows the
deliverable itself: an artifact in the artifact panel (file preview, deck
viewer), a report as the conversation's own `report_to_user` card in a dialog,
a merge at its Worker's result Turn. The secondary action ("Go to message")
opens the lead's message at that exact Turn. In the classic view the items
live on the lead's task board ("Each task expands to its reports and files"
below); neither the sidebar nor the conversation's right-hand panel holds a
deliverables list. Each row shows a type glyph (report kind, deck, PDF,
document, sheet, image, file, merge), the whole title wrapped at word
boundaries, then its kind in the glyph's ink, project and relative time,
previews the report's opening paragraph on hover, and opens its attached files
from chips (owner request 2026-11-17: the sidebar list was cramped, titles cut
mid-word). In the studio, a delivery on the desk and a trophy on the shelf build the
same item (same identity) and call the same opener, whose Turn port docks the
office's conversation column; other desk documents still open their author's brief.

Alternatives rejected: a pointer ledger written by each producer (the first
implementation; a parallel store that needed seeding and could disagree with
the reports and artifacts it copied); scraping Turns in the browser (only
covers loaded windows); deriving the list from the latest-report head (it keeps
only the newest report); a second studio-only implementation.

#### Each task expands to its reports and files (owner request 2026-11-17)

Problem: the sidebar section listed every report and file of a lead by time,
apart from the work it belonged to, and duplicated the task board in the
narrow sidebar; the owner asked to drop it and read each task's results by
expanding that task on the board.

Decision: `report_to_user` takes `tasks`, the titles of the board tasks the
report is about; one report may cover several. Each resolves to `{key, title}`,
where `key` is the task's history identity (`progressHistoryKey`, "Task
history"), by its current or a former title on the board or in its history,
then ignoring case and spacing. The call's receipt names them, as it names the
files, and the relay notice carries them (`Tasks:` line) to the Global Router.
The deliverables projection gives each report its `tasks`; a page filters by
`task` (a key, or `other`) and always summarises the conversation: `tasks` (key,
latest title, count, newest time) and `otherCount`. Only a task of the lead's
own project counts as a task: reports the Global Router relays from projects
keep their `tasks` for display but read under `other`. `progressState.taskKeys`
gives the browser each board task's key.

#### Automatic work reports and report links (owner request 2026-11-17)

Problem: after the board change no task had anything to expand: every existing
report predated task names, and nothing tied a completed task to its result.
Making a final report mandatory for completion would turn a forgotten report
into repeated tool refusals in a lead that runs for hours.

Decision: naming tasks stays optional and never refuses a report. A title that
matches no task (or a history read that fails) publishes the report unlinked
and the receipt says which titles matched nothing (`unlinked_tasks`). Each
completed board task that no delivery report names gets an automatic work
report: a `completion` item projected from its task history record (final
progress note, completion time), read at request time, so it costs the lead no
call and cannot fail. A delivery report naming the task replaces it. Reports
published before tasks could be named are attached afterwards by report links
(`coordinator_report_links.py`, `reportLink`, namespace
`reporting.linkNamespace`): durable, idempotent, one report to any number of
tasks, never editing the report. The owner (or an agent acting for the owner)
links them by title through `POST /api/v4/project/coordination/report-links`;
only canonical report Turns of the lead's own conversation are linked, with the
same lenient title rule. The main lead's earlier reports are linked this way by
an agent's judgement over their titles and the task history.

Alternatives rejected: refusing `update_task_progress` to complete a task
without a delivery report (repeated tool failures and wasted rounds, the
owner's concern); writing a synthetic report Turn at completion (a second
transcript entry the lead never wrote, and a write that can fail inside the
board CAS); matching old reports to tasks by text similarity at read time
(silent mislinks with no way to correct them).

On the board (`features/coordinator-progress.ts` with the lazily loaded
`coordinator-task-deliverables.ts`), a task with items is a disclosure showing
its count; expanding it lists that task's items newest first
(`lead-deliverables/rows.ts`, older pages on demand). The summary joins its
keys with the history it already reads: current in-progress and pending rows
lead, then tasks removed unfinished, then folded completed/cancelled rows
(including earlier tasks with items). An off-board item with no known history
says "Status unknown". Task history still lists tasks without any items.
Each board row names its state in text. "Marked complete" is the lead's
recorded status, not a verification verdict. A separate delivery-report label
reports whether a delivery report is explicitly linked; it implies neither
verification nor owner acceptance. An automatic board note is labeled as such
and never counts as a delivery report. No title matching, assignment read,
or new store is used. Other items (unlinked reports, files published on their
own, relayed reports) stay reachable under "Other reports and files". A
`lead_deliverable` push, reconnect or newer board revision re-reads the summary
and each open task. A task's list starts reading
when the pointer enters or focus reaches its row, so it is usually there by the
click; a list read ahead but not opened is read again on opening after a
push, reconnect or newer board revision. Invalidation aborts and detaches a
closed list's in-flight request: even a response that arrives despite abort
cannot replace the newer summary or supply that opening. Opening or folding
without invalidation keeps the same read-ahead request. Open-list refreshes
still coalesce a follow-up read and retain older pages. Each task remembers the
last successful first-page continuation separately from its advanced More
cursor. A changed first-page continuation resets More, even after exhaustion;
an unchanged one keeps the advanced cursor and adds no continuation reads.
Continuation rows join immediately after the last fetched page's item ID,
before retained older rows, deduplicating overlaps without timestamp comparisons
or replacing the untouched older interval. This keeps newly exposed gap reports
reachable across tied timestamps without rewinding unchanged lists. If the
existing completion filtering removes that boundary item, its nearest surviving
predecessor in the fetched interval keeps the insertion boundary; the advanced
cursor is unchanged, and continuation rows still precede untouched older rows.
The latest summary of each project's lead stays in the page, so a board
mounted again for that lead (a project switch back) shows its counts at once
while the fresh summary reads; one entry per project. The sidebar's deliverables
section and its bridge are deleted; the lead's entry keeps its unread report
preview.

The same evening a delivery moved the list from the sidebar into the context
card of the lead conversation's latest round (the right-hand panel); the owner
then asked for the board instead, to remove the redundant section, so that
mount is deleted and its row design is the board's (`lead-deliverables/rows.ts`).

Alternatives rejected: matching reports to tasks by title text in the browser
(titles change and collide; the key is the history identity); keeping the
sidebar list, or the right-hand panel list, beside the board (two places to
look, the owner's complaint);
moving every item onto the board with no "other" group (older reports would
vanish); a per-task deliverables store (the projection already holds the
facts).

### Office team facts (2026-11-13)

Problem: the office must show who works, who walks in with a report, which
deliverable lands, which owner question waits, the board and an alarm, each
keyed by the conversation, assignment, message and Turn it comes from. None of
these reached a browser as a push:

- **No busy edge.** Every member hint re-read a busy floor's whole team page;
  each re-read also read the owner's whole active-job list, and GAME-UI-1
  measured about six re-reads per second per busy floor.
- **Producer hints never arrive.** Coordinator and Worker tools run in the
  resident execution host. `push_event` reaches only the writing process's
  sockets under the default inproc bus, so a hint published where a tool writes
  a fact never arrives (as "One progress source" above records for the board).
- **Polled windows miss facts.** Results were found by diffing the 32-row
  assignment page, and questions by reading a 20-Turn sync window.

The only change signal that crosses processes is the conversation commit watch.

Decision: one owner-scoped, read-only feed in the API host,
`lib/router/office_facts.py`. It is derived from the existing owners and has no
state machine of its own.

- **Trigger.** The commit watch's scopes (`broker.add_scope_listener`) start a
  pass for an owner whose browser holds an `office` push subscription, or that
  just read the snapshot. No timer, interval, polling loop or setting exists.
  One thread runs passes, and scopes arriving during a pass join that owner's
  next one (single flight).
- **Nobody watching.** A trigger that finds no subscription and no pending
  snapshot read parks the owner's baseline: it is kept (one per owner, with its
  facts, watermark, placements, mailbox versions and suspected attempts), and no
  pass or attempt check runs for it.
  `project` hints it misses are kept with it. The next snapshot read, or a
  trigger once a socket subscribes again, catches it up with one watch-gap pass
  from the kept watermark (below), on the same `streamId`: its changes go out
  as ordinary frames, so a reader that kept its facts stays in order. That pass
  also reads each lead's newest history page, since a Worker whose attempt
  began and ended while nobody watched left no busy edge to find its result on.
  Re-entering the office therefore costs what changed while away, never a
  cold rebuild.
- **Watch gaps.** The watch names only commits made while it waits; under load
  many batches are gaps. A gap starts a pass for every active owner, and the
  catalog's recency index (`conversation.list` updated at or after a watermark
  taken from the rows' own clock) names the conversations that committed. If
  that read fails or fills the operation's page maximum, every conversation
  counts as committed.
- **Derivation.** A pass is an exact diff of two consecutive reads of the
  narrow authority:
  - `busy` is a conversation whose attempt runs now (`running_conversation_tasks`:
    the durable `running` jobs and live local tasks behind `list_running_tasks`,
    never queued, suspended or pending work).
    - An attempt's terminal commit precedes its job's completion, and that
      completion commits nothing. So a running attempt whose conversation
      committed becomes a suspect, and its attempt is read while the feed has
      no pass to run. The reads stop as soon as a trigger arrives: under load,
      the next pass re-reads the running jobs anyway.
    - A settled suspect ends its busy fact at once, even before its job
      completes.
  - its placement (project, assignment, lead) comes once per attempt from the
    accepted config's dispatch admission;
  - `report` has three sources:
    - a lead's newest coordinator report from its report head (`unread` from
      the read position, never the Turn body). A read marked over HTTP commits
      no conversation, so the API host's own `project` hint (a Push Hub
      publication listener) refreshes that lead.
    - a pending `kind=report` project message in a lead's mailbox, cleared once
      the lead's round confirms it.
    - a Worker's completed, unreviewed assignment result, found on a busy-to-idle
      edge through the attempt's assignment association, and at the baseline on
      each lead's newest history page (`coverage.reports: newest_page`). It is
      cleared when its lead reviews it at that revision, or when the assignment
      is revoked or rebound.
    - The page is read once per lead (`StoragePorts.newest_results`). A
      reviewed row is final (`record_review` is immutable and
      `continue_execution` refuses a reviewed association), so it costs no read
      and never yields another result. An unreviewed row whose attempt runs has
      no result yet. Any other unreviewed row costs one Turn metadata read: it
      carries the Turn's current attempt, status, revision and ordinal, and the
      attempt is read only when the Turn has moved on to another attempt.
    - The busy-to-idle edge (`StoragePorts.result`) decides its one row by the
      same rule (`_unreviewed`), so the live feed and a cold read agree.
  - `decision` is an owner question a lead asked (`ask_human`, recorded in the
    R10 owner-question index) while the index says it is open
    (`open_questions`). It is read from the whole index, never a Turn window,
    and carries the Turn ordinal so a click opens the exact Turn. Answers are
    recorded on the index when they happen (docs/ROUTER.md R10, "Owner question
    answers are recorded when they happen"), so a pass reads no lead lane: an
    answered or open non-blocking question costs no Turn read, and an open
    blocking question one read of its card.
  - `document` is a Worker result its lead accepted. It is set when an
    unreviewed result's review is `accept`, and at the baseline from each
    lead's newest history page. A document is never withdrawn.
  - `board` is a lead's task board revision. `alarm` is an open Router Watchdog
    signal, re-read only when the Watchdog's conversation commits: every new
    signal wakes it, and it resolves signals in its own rounds.
  - `coverage` names a source a build does not derive yet `unsupported`.
- **Wire.** The snapshot `GET /api/v4/router/office` is the feed's baseline
  under the same lock as `seq`. Deltas are `office.fact` frames on the existing
  push socket, `set` or `cleared` per stable key (`busy:<conversationId>`,
  `report:<lead>:<turnId>`, `message:<messageId>`,
  `result:<assignmentId>:<revision>`, `document:<assignmentId>:<revision>`,
  `question:<conversationId>:<guidanceId>`, `board:<projectPath>`,
  `alarm:<signalId>`). Frames carry identifiers only, never a tool call, title
  or transcript text; content stays on the existing GETs.
- **Ordering and resume.**
  - `seq` rises by one per frame within a `streamId` (process boot plus the
    owner's baseline generation). A parked baseline keeps its `streamId`.
  - A reader re-reads the snapshot on a gap, on a new `streamId` or after a
    push reconnect. That repairs a reconnect, a server restart (a new boot
    rebuilds the baseline from the authority) and a lost frame.
  - The first pass publishes nothing: the snapshot carries it.
- **Authorization.** The snapshot passes the existing middleware
  (`require_scope('conversations')`, explicit owner). Frames pass Push Hub owner
  filtering and the browser's frame-owner check. A non-owner receives nothing.

Agreed with GAME-UI-2 (the office's integrator) and HARNESS-1C (RouterAttention).
Where they overlap, the keys are the attention ids.

Alternatives considered:

- **Hints at the producers** (a `busy` field on `conv_changed`;
  `assignment_result`, `owner_question` and enriched `coordinator_report`
  `project` frames). They are published in the execution host and never
  arrive.
- **An execution-to-API push relay.** A second transport and lifecycle, still
  without ordering or resume.
- **The office subscribing to every Worker's Conversation Sync stream.** It
  ships every tool round's patch, and holds N streams.
- **A durable team-event log.** A parallel state machine and new persistent
  bytes.
- **A server-side periodic read.** A polling loop with an interval knob.
- **Keeping client re-reads.** About six team-page reads per second per busy
  floor per tab.
- **Dropping a baseline nobody watches** (until 2026-11-14). Every office
  re-entry then paid a cold rebuild (13-20 s on production); a kept baseline
  costs one owner's facts in memory and catches up with what changed.
- **A time-to-live on parked baselines, or passes kept running for them.** A
  tuned lifetime (principle 5), or reads nobody looks at.

Specifications: `tests/router/test_office_facts.py` and the route cases in
`tests/api/test_api_v4_router.py`. They cover:

- busy only while an attempt runs, and queued, suspended or pending work never
  busy;
- a terminal commit ends busy before the job completes, also inside a watch gap,
  and attempt reads yield to pending passes;
- a report only for a real report (lead report heads, `kind=report` messages,
  completed unreviewed results) and its clearing (read, taken, reviewed);
- a decision only for an open owner question, cleared on its answer and never
  windowed;
- a document only for an accepted result;
- a board for its revision and an alarm while a Watchdog signal is open;
- no frame without a change, and never for a tool call;
- contiguous order;
- gap, reconnect and restart resumption;
- a gap in the watch;
- a parked baseline: no read while nobody watches, one gap pass from its
  watermark on the same stream, equal to a cold rebuild, including results of
  Workers that ran meanwhile, missed `project` hints and attempts suspected
  before it parked; a failed catch-up stays parked; one baseline per owner;
- one history page per lead, no attempt read for a reviewed, running or
  settled row, and the busy-to-idle edge decided by the same rule;
- owner questions read from the index without a lane read
  (`tests/router/test_owner_question_reads.py`, production shape);
- unreadable sources keep their facts;
- non-owners get nothing, through the real Push Hub and the route's
  authentication;
- the broker listener seam.

The recorded snapshot and frame sequences for browser reducer specs (a gap, a
restart) are `tests/fixtures/office_facts/stream_v1.json`. The same test file
checks that fixture against the contract.

### Proactive reports and durable attention

Problem: an unseen result in a Worker chat or a volatile notification cannot
reliably tell the human that the Coordinator needs a decision or has delivered.
Decision: the bound Coordinator's `report_to_user` tool publishes a concise
`progress`, `decision`, `blocked` or `delivery` report as an ordinary assistant
Turn in its own conversation. The title is at most 120 characters; the plain
text message is at most 4,000. Role guidance asks for meaningful milestones and
verified delivery, rather than every status change; questions are `ask_human`
cards, never reports (`decision` remains readable for recorded history).
Reporting grants no authority and never implies human acknowledgement or consent.

Minimal human interruption (owner decision 2026-10-26). The system asks the
human only when progress needs something only the human can provide and no
sensible default exists. Pending reviews, unmet dependencies, retries,
re-delegation, and merging/publishing verified work are the Coordinator's to
resolve; role guidance and the `report_to_user` description forbid presenting
them as questions or permission requests. The human can still intervene at any
time by talking to the Coordinator or any Worker. Alternative rejected: a
derived "needs your attention" list of review/dependency/delivery items. It
turned the Coordinator's internal queue into human chores, and it offered no
action the human could take.

`coordinator_reports.py` first retains an immutable publication intent, then
uses a stable canonical Turn command, updates one latest-report/read pointer,
and retires the transient intent. The existing project startup recovery pages
through pending intents; it never invokes a model or creates a task. Lost
acknowledgements, concurrent identical sends and restarts reuse the same Turn.
A changed request under an existing key is rejected without poisoning later
original retries. Older retries cannot replace newer attention or rearm read
messages. Owner, project, Coordinator identity and binding revision are checked;
replacement withdraws pending transport while preserving old conversation history.
Reports can be ingested during the ordinary Coordinator attempt without replacing
that attempt or preventing its settlement.

The attention head is a projection, not an independent transcript. If its Turn
has been deleted, reads return empty attention after rechecking the binding;
they do not rewrite history or the read watermark. Storage failures and a
present Turn with mismatched identity still fail visibly. Treating a missing
Turn as an internal error was rejected because a legitimate history deletion
would break every subsequent report poll.

Model view. Problem: a report Turn is ingested after the still-running reply
that published it. Replaying it as one more assistant message after that reply
makes every later request carry assistant/assistant adjacency, which
`build_body` merges with an "UNEXPECTED pair" warning, and the model reads each
report twice: as its own `report_to_user` arguments and appended to the reply's
final text, so a cold rebuild no longer matches the view the model sent.
Decision: report Turns are human-facing delivery records, not model output.
History projection (`_transform_messages`; the Turn-native builder projects
`_turnKind` as the conversation projection does) omits every
`coordinator_report.*` Turn. The model keeps each report through the publishing
reply's own `report_to_user` call and result (title, message, report Turn ID).
The deterministic stall-stop report was never a model turn either; the next
human Turn carries the steering. Alternatives rejected: declaring a designed
same-role seam silences the guard but keeps the duplicated, misplaced text;
omitting only reports matched to a visible call needs parsing compactable tool
results or arguments across restored prefixes and still leaves unmatched
reports adjacent; projecting reports as user-role notices adds a prompt surface
that recasts the model's own words; not creating report Turns breaks report
receipts, unread pointers and the UI. The final `llm_sanitize` merge remains the
last-resort guard and still warns for any other adjacent assistant producer.
Verification: `tests/tasks_pkg/test_coordinator_report_model_view.py` (real
report producer and Turn-native builder, cold and restored replay).

Reading order. Problem: storage appends a `coordinator_report.*` Turn after the
lane tail, usually the still-running reply that published it, and names that
tail its `parentTurnId`. In durable order the report trailed the live reply and
every later call it made, under the project-brain role label (its origin stamp)
and the reply's Answer toolbar, so it read as a newer answer from another
persona (owner report 2026-11-11). The earlier placement moved only reports
without a parent, so it never moved a stored report.
Decision: presentation (`presentation/view-model/report-placement.ts`) folds
each report into the reply that published it: the reply whose `report_to_user`
result names the report Turn ID or, while that result is in flight, the reply
whose execution window covers it (reply live, or report `createdAt` < attempt
`settledAt`). The reply gains a `coordinator-report` block right after that
call. The grouped layout keeps the block in the reply's text area, after the
tool list and above the live status, never inside the panel's fold or call
window. `ui/renderers/coordinator-report-block.ts` renders a labeled card,
"Coordinator report · Progress/Decision/Blocked/Delivery" (负责人汇报 ·
进展/决定/受阻/交付), with the report's own time, copy and translation and no Answer
toolbar. A report published outside every reply stays its own Turn with the
same label and no Answer toolbar. Placement reads only durable facts, so nothing
moves when the reply settles; a changed report Turn repaints its host reply.
Durable lane order, the server's `brain` origin stamp (continuation checks it)
and the read pointer (the server's latest report Turn ID) are unchanged.
Alternatives rejected: reading the report above its reply leaves the reply's
later calls and steered messages beneath an older message; a card inside the
tool list is hidden whenever the panel is folded or windowed; splitting the
reply into before/after-report bubbles needs two view models per Turn identity
across windowing, navigation and actions; restamping the report's initiator
breaks continuation's provenance check. Verification:
`frontend/src/conversation/presentation/coordinator-report-placement.test.ts`,
`frontend/src/conversation/ui/renderers/coordinator-report-block.test.ts` and
the browser journey `tests/frontend/test_coordinator_report_browser.py`.

Folded reports. Problem: a Coordinator reply runs for hours and publishes
several reports, and every card read open in full, so each report's whole
message lengthened the live reply (owner report 2026-11-12).
Decision: each card in a reply is a native disclosure folded to one row in the
reply's process-fold grammar: report label, the report's title, its time and a
trailing chevron. The title is the first line of the displayed text
(`report_to_user` publishes `title\n\nmessage`), so a translated report shows
its translated title. The card is created folded; from then on the open state
is the reader's, and no repaint of the live reply opens or folds it. Opened,
the row keeps its geometry and hides the title, which the body starts with. A
report published outside every reply is a message of its own and stays open.

Reports are what the owner reads (owner request 2026-11-17, superseding the
2026-11-13 decision that reports are process descriptions in the narration
well). Problem: in the main coordinator's conversation (95 Turns, 2026-11-17)
26 automatic continuation briefs held 104.5k characters, more than every reply
together, the 25 reports were 22% of the text and sat folded inside the
narration well between 1,000+ tool calls, and files the leads produced were
named only by paths inside report prose, so the owner could not find the
deliverables. Decision:

- A report never joins the narration well (`interimNarrationIds`); a grouped
  reply reads its reports after its prose, in their own order
  (`groupedTurnLayout`), so no fold or later round text sits between the owner
  and a report.
- A `delivery`, `decision` or `blocked` card is created open, a `progress`
  card folded; afterwards the open state stays the reader's.
- `report_to_user` takes `files` (project paths). The tool publishes each one
  through the `publish_response_file` publisher (verified roots, supported
  types) before the report, all or none, and its receipt names them as
  `reportFile`s (`project_coordination_v1.schema.json`). The report card reads
  them from its publishing call's receipt and shows them as chips below its
  head, outside the fold, each opening the file in the artifact panel
  (`open-artifact`). The Turn projection is unchanged: the receipt is the
  record, so no conversation-sync or engine field was added.
- A project-brain `virtual_user` input (an automatic continuation, a delegated
  brief) reads as one folded row, "Automatic instruction" and its first line
  (`renderers/machine-brief-block.ts`); its body renders only when opened.
- Each board task expands to its reports and files ("Each task expands to its
  reports and files"); the sidebar holds no deliverables list.

Alternatives rejected: a separate "for me" transcript mode that hides process
Turns (a second view model per Turn identity across windowing, navigation and
actions, and the owner would have to switch modes to find a report); parsing
paths out of report prose (unreliable, and files outside a published artifact
cannot be opened); a new Turn projection field for the files (needs a
conversation-sync contract and TofuDB engine change for data the receipt
already records); opening the newest report only (folds the previous one under
the reader as each report arrives). Verification: the renderer, placement and
grouped-layout suites, `machine-brief-block.test.ts`, and the browser journey
named above.

The sidebar's fixed Coordinator entry shows an unread dot and the latest title.
Clicking it opens the ordinary conversation, where the report is a normal
message. Opening the Coordinator chat while the page is visible reads the latest
message, as in any messaging app (owner decision 2026-11-02); a hidden tab reads
it when it becomes visible and a failed read retries on the next visibility or
online signal. The read command captures the displayed Turn ID and binding
revision; a stale tab reading A cannot clear newer B. The sidebar, another
conversation of the project and fetching alone never acknowledge. Failed refreshes retain known
attention only within the exact binding, show a retry, and do not invent read
state. Project/conversation push events are refresh hints; reconnect/reload reads
the durable projection. Full report history remains solely in canonical Turns.
No new timer, subscription transport, worker or polling model call is introduced.

Alternatives rejected: local-storage badges lose cross-session state; clearing
the latest pointer without its exact Turn ID can discard unseen concurrent
reports; an explicit "mark as read" control made the human manage a state the
open chat already resolves; a separate inbox transcript duplicates the
conversation authority. Verification:
report race/recovery specifications, real registered tool pipeline and HTTP
journeys, SQLite and source-matched native restart/active-attempt checks, and a
real browser journey covering push while another chat is open, exact reading,
reload, three themes and widths down to 320 px. Storage uses one `project.coordinator.reports.v1` head per
owner/project and `project.coordinator.reports.pending.v1` intents until confirmed
publication or binding revocation. The existing recovery lifecycle reads at most
32 intents per page; it adds no resident queue or cache. UTF-8 shape measurement
with a 4,096-character project path, 256-character conversation/Turn IDs,
120-character title, 4,000-character body and 200-character retry key measured
17,557 bytes for the head and 34,069 per intent (four-byte characters in
path/title/body/key). These are record-shape measurements, not latency/RSS claims.
The 33-intent recovery test verifies page advancement and retirement to one head;
completion deletes transport records, never messages.

### Stable capabilities, discoverable conversations and diagnosis

#### Session execution control

Problem: a Coordinator could read transcripts and manage its own assignments,
but could not inspect or operate a concrete existing conversation as the owner
could through Continue and Stop. Decision: `get_conversation_status` and
`control_conversation` share `lib/conversations/session_control.py`. The Global
Router can address the owner's conversations; a project Coordinator addresses
conversations in its project. The live binding and owner are checked on every
call. The Watchdog and ordinary Workers receive neither control capability.

Status never reads a whole conversation. Problem: it read the complete
synchronization snapshot and filtered `turn_id` afterwards, so a long history
failed with `database_resource_exhausted` ("turn response exceeds its bounded
streamed payload") even for one Turn, and a medium one spilled every Turn into
an artifact. Decision: an explicit `turn_id` reads that Turn's metadata and its
current Attempt by identity. Otherwise one lane (default `main`) is read
newest-first through `turn.sync.page`, each page one bounded read that the
repository reads again smaller when the authority refuses it at its response
bound, and Turns are admitted while the result still reaches the model inline
under the single tool-result budget (`fits_tool_result_budget` in
`lib/tasks_pkg/compaction/_budget.py`). The newest Turn is always shown;
`window.next_before_ordinal` with `lane_id` pages older Turns. Both forms expose
Turn/Attempt identities, revisions, settlement/resume options and the queue
preview without execution config; the live presentation phase and replay head
stay browser concerns. `get_conversation` remains the transcript reader. Status
is passive and is never cached as authorization. Alternatives rejected: a fixed
Turn count per window (either spills heavy settlements into an artifact or hides
Turns that fit), `turn.sync.snapshot` with `turn_limit` (bounded only for linear
conversations; a branched one still takes the whole-history path), and a new
metadata-only page operation (a new semantic operation on both authorities,
while page reads already stay inside the existing bound). Specification:
`tests/conversation/test_coordinator_status_bound.py` (SQL and native, a history
beyond a 96 KiB response bound).

Partial results are readable by every Coordinator role. A result above the
single tool-result budget becomes a partial envelope whose only recovery is
`read_tool_artifact`/`search_tool_artifact` (kept eager in the registry for
exactly this reason), yet `COORDINATOR_TOOLS` (and so `ROUTER_TOOLS`) and
`WATCHDOG_TOOLS` lacked them: a Coordinator got an `artifactRef` it could not
read. The readers are now in all three ceilings; they only page results the
role already received. Coordinator list reads additionally return a first page
the model reads inline (see `get_project_workers` and project message
directories), so the reader is the fallback for other oversized results
(`get_conversation`, `get_project_assignment`), not the normal path.

Control supports `continue`, `checkpoint_resume`, `regenerate` and exact-attempt
`stop`. It validates the current authorized Coordinator request through the
existing delegation boundary, then reuses the conversation command service used
by the browser. Resumes retain the target's accepted model/config and identity;
they add no human message, answer, approval or enlarged tool grant. Dispatch
attempts retain their exact `TrustedDispatch` admission.

**A lead holds a Worker without losing it (owner request 2026-11-18).**
Problem: a lead's `stop` is final for a dispatched Worker. Stopping its Attempt
withdraws the conversation's dispatch (`turn_lifecycle.abort_attempt` →
`revoke_conversation_dispatch`), so a later `continue` fails with "Grant
revoked" (assignment 215f1bf0, conversation worker-d51b97d5). During the
2026-11-18 AppId …5409 quota pressure the parallel lead needed to throttle
lanes. It could only stop them, which lost the Workers, or re-delegate them.
