Decision: `control_conversation` gains the pair `hold` / `resume`, built only
from the existing owners. No new state or store is added:

- `hold` (no `expected_revision`) applies only to a Worker session a lead
  dispatched (`turn_dispatch` is trusted). It passes the same
  `worker_control(session=True)` gate and the same audit as `stop`. It stops the
  exact current Attempt through the Stop entry point with the dispatch withdrawal
  off and the abort reason `lead_hold` (`ConversationTurnCommandService.hold_project_attempt`,
  the counterpart of `abort_project_attempt`). That reason is a row of
  `turn_verdict.ABORT_CAUSES` (initiator `LEAD_HOLD`). The Turn settles
  `interrupted` with cause `lead_hold`, and settlement offers `continue` even
  before any progress, as for `server_restart` (`PAUSE_SETTLEMENT_CAUSES`).
  `PAUSE_ABORT_REASONS` gives a hold the same handling as a restart's quiesce.
  A cut call is interrupted and repeatable, never "do not resume". The repeatable
  cut batch is dropped from the resume replay. Tool results and waits say "the
  project lead's hold … the project dispatch grant is unchanged". The grant
  revision, checkout (work id) and assignment are untouched. Holding a held
  Turn again is an `alreadyTerminal` receipt. A queued or settled Attempt is
  refused.
- `resume` takes the held Turn's `currentAttemptId` and `projectionRevision`.
  It runs the same `create_attempt` command `continue`/`checkpoint_resume` use,
  with the operation and anchor the settlement offers (`continue` first, then
  `checkpoint_resume`, `restart_resume.RESUME_OPERATIONS`). It keeps the
  `TrustedDispatch` admission, so the assignment advances in place
  (`continue_execution`). A lost ACK replays the started Attempt. A Turn that no
  lead held is refused with a pointer to its own resume options. A revoked grant
  (owner Stop, `cancel_project_work`, owner withdrawal) refuses the resume
  through the existing admission. Holding never protects a Worker from
  revocation.
- Held is derived, not stored. `project_dispatch.continuation.execution_status`
  reads `held` from an interrupted Attempt whose Turn cause is `lead_hold`. The
  Turn metadata is read only for interrupted Attempts. Coordinator views
  (`get_project_workers`, `poll`, wake summaries) report status `held` and
  phase `on_hold`. `held` stays the delivery-hold phase. `on_hold` is the lead's
  own act: it is never news and never wakes the Coordinator, and a `poll`
  lists it under `held`. It is still actionable: the request stays open, and
  continuation context reminds the lead to resume or cancel it. The Worker's
  Turn chrome reads "Held", and the tool cards label status `held` and the
  `hold`/`resume` actions.
- Under the owner's delegation (Act-for-me) `hold` and `resume` are admitted
  beside `continue`, `checkpoint_resume`, `stop` and `set_model`, from any wake
  kind. The owner's own Stop, `stop`, and revocation keep withdrawing the grant
  unchanged. Project dispatch restart recovery resumes only `server_restart`
  interruptions, so a held Worker stays held across a restart until its lead
  resumes it.

Alternatives rejected: (1) Make a lead's `stop` keep the grant. That changes
the owner-visible Stop semantics, and leads also use `stop` to end work for
good. (2) A hold flag or paused status in the assignment record or a scheduler
queue. That is a parallel state machine beside the Turn settlement that both
the executor and the restart recovery would have to keep consistent. (3) Hold
the lane by deferring the Worker's next model call (a gate in admission). It
keeps a live task and lease during the pause and adds a wake/poll mechanism.
(4) Re-delegate on resume. That loses the checkout, the account and the
assignment identity the review pins. Specification:
`tests/router/test_lead_delegation.py`
(`test_a_wake_holds_its_worker_and_resumes_it_under_the_same_assignment_and_grant`,
real sqlite and native authorities, commitment, project-message and Watchdog
wakes under Act-for-me), `tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_session_control.py`
(hold/resume unit contract) and `tests/turn_lifecycle/test_lead_hold_settlement.py`.

**Worker control is scoped by the Coordinator binding (owner follow-up
2026-11-15; review and cancel 2026-11-16).** Problem: every server restart interrupted several Workers with
`worker_effect_outcome_unknown`; each waited for its Coordinator's
`checkpoint_resume`, and when the Coordinator was running as an automatic
continuation it was refused ("Automatic continuation may control only its
original request workers") because those Workers came from an earlier steered
request of the same Coordinator. Recovery stalled until the owner wrote.
Decision: `control_conversation` (`checkpoint_resume`, `continue`,
`regenerate`, `stop`) and `follow_up` of a dispatched Worker session pass
`worker_control(..., session=True)`: any Turn of the Coordinator conversation,
human-steered or an automatic continuation, controls the sessions of every
Worker its own binding dispatched, in any request of its project. The owner,
tenant, Coordinator-identity and binding-epoch fences still apply, so another
Coordinator's Workers, another project's and the Global Router's are refused as
before; the owner's delegation keeps its own gate (a lead's cross-project
action, "A lead acts on another project's sessions under the delegation"). Every
`control_conversation` call, accepted or refused, writes one
`coordinator_session_control` audit record (owner, project, Coordinator
conversation/task/Turn, principal kind, action, target conversation/Turn/
Attempt, outcome, refusal reason). Acting on a session neither creates nor
widens a grant: it resumes or stops the execution the Worker's grant already
runs under.

`review_project_work` and `cancel_project_work` pass the same
`worker_control(..., session=True)` gate. Problem (2026-11-16, Coordinator
coordinator-4950501b): the automatic continuation of a Coordinator's first
request was refused review of a finished Worker the same Coordinator had
dispatched in a later, owner-forwarded request, so the result waited behind
that request's own wake or the owner's next message ("finished work waits
behind others"). Decision: any Turn of the Coordinator conversation reviews or
cancels any Worker its binding dispatched in any request of its project. Each
call, accepted or refused, writes one `coordinator_session_control` record
(`action` `review_project_work`/`cancel_project_work`, `assignment_id`,
`decision`, `checkpoint_sha`, outcome, refusal reason; same Coordinator fields).
Another Coordinator's Workers are refused by the Coordinator-identity fence;
another project's assignment is not in this binding's project repository. A
review changes no account or policy of the reviewing request, and the accept's
publication stays the reviewed assignment's own: its pin subject is that
assignment's original delegation (`coordinator:<its taskId>`) and
`validate_publication` checks it against that assignment's grant, so the
delivery publishes instead of holding `project_publication_authority_missing`.
The request-scoped `worker_control` (no `session`) remains only for a nonhuman
principal acting under the Router's owner delegation.

Alternatives rejected: rebinding a
continuation to every earlier request (one reply would carry several requests'
accounts and policies); requiring an owner message to reach the continuation
("An owner message in an automatic continuation controls every Worker of its
project": exactly the manual step this removes); keeping review and cancel
request-scoped (a finished Worker of another request then waits for that
request's continuation); widening to the Global Router
or across projects (outside the owner's delegation to his own Coordinator).
Specifications: `tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_worker_handoff.py`
(`test_a_continuation_controls_the_sessions_of_its_coordinators_earlier_request_workers`,
`test_session_control_of_another_coordinators_worker_stays_refused_and_is_audited`,
`test_automatic_continuation_reviews_and_cancels_another_requests_workers_of_its_binding`,
`test_review_and_cancel_of_another_coordinators_or_projects_worker_stay_refused_and_audited`)
and `tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_resumed_publication.py::test_a_continuation_publishes_its_review_of_another_requests_worker`.
Ordinary session resumes retain their existing human-authorized execution.
Nonhuman notifications without an authorized request cannot issue controls,
with one exception: the Global Router acting under the owner's delegation
(owner decision 2026-11-06, [ROUTER.md R9](ROUTER.md#r9-the-owners-delegation-the-global-router-acts-for-the-owner-owner-decision-2026-11-06)).
When its request has no owner message of its own (a standing instruction, a
nonhuman notice), `control_conversation(owner_delegation={reason, trigger})`
checks the owner's grant in the lifecycle approval gate, resolves the owner
trigger, records one audit action before the command and settles it with the
outcome (a refused trigger is recorded too). The command is the unchanged
`control_session`: the delegation stands in only for the current request's
source principal (`acting_principal`), so every Attempt, revision, tenant and
replay check still applies and the resumed Attempt keeps the target's own
accepted execution identity. It allows only `continue`, `checkpoint_resume` and
`stop` of the pinned Attempt and `set_model` of its session (below), in any
project of the owner, never `regenerate`; a
dispatched Worker keeps its exact grant. Passing `owner_delegation` always uses
the grant; without it the owner's own message keeps the unchanged path.
Delegated Continue is how the Router resumes the exact Attempts a restart
interrupted (`tests/conversation/test_coordinator_session_control.py` covers
it on SQL and native storage after boot recovery settled them).

`set_model` changes only the model of an existing session, preserving its
conversation, grant, checkout, tool checkpoint and authorization scope
(owner decision 2026-11-09; [ROUTER.md R5](ROUTER.md#r5-router-and-worker-models-are-chosen-separately)).
`lib/conversations/session_model.py` stores the owner's route-admitted choice as
the server-authored `modelOverride` conversation setting; the named Attempt's
accepted execution tenant is checked, and a lead without a current owner
request in scope needs the owner's live delegation to route a session. Only
`set_model` can omit the authority object while using the full standing grant:
the server resolves authority through the same R9 checks and uses the top-level
`reason` for audit. The next accepted attempt resolves the override in `TrustedDispatch.capture`;
the running attempt keeps the model it was accepted with. `capture` resolves the
conversation's configured thinking depth the same way (`apply_session_policy`),
so every Turn of a conversation runs at the effort its owner set, whichever
request's grant it continues (CONTEXT_COMPACTION.md, "Dispatch Turns inherit the
conversation's effort"). `_restored_identity`
admits only the grant's model, the session's current choice or the model the
bound attempt's durable accepted config names, so a carrier rewritten to any
other model loses its authority. New Worker grants (including reviewers and
final-result follow-up successors) use the current Coordinator selection per
[ROUTER.md R5](ROUTER.md#r5-router-and-worker-models-are-chosen-separately);
an in-place follow-up keeps its grant unless the recipient has a session override.
The change and its
reason are recorded in `audit.log` (`session_model_changed`), and a lead's
delegated call takes the same R9 path as a delegated Continue, its
audit record naming the chosen model. `model=null` clears the override:
Coordinator continuations use the saved composer choice if present, and other
dispatch attempts use their grant's model. No provider refusal or tool output is
rewritten.

Callers pin the source Attempt, Turn revision and idempotency key. A retry uses
the same command identity and stored source config; conflicts require a fresh
status read, never a silently selected successor. A `checkpoint_resume` anchor
may be echoed without its derived `retainedToolRoundPositions` list: the
authority then substitutes the settlement's own list rather than deriving one
from the projection (deriving would keep superseded provider attempts). That
shorthand selects the same durable rounds and resumes, while any explicit
difference is still a typed conflict that writes nothing. See `docs/contracts/TURN_SETTLEMENT.md#resume-options`. Stop targets only the named
current Attempt and uses the ordinary Stop/withdrawal owner; a terminal Attempt
is a no-op so replay cannot withdraw newer work. Resuming an earlier Turn has the
same later-history removal semantics as the browser command. A lost mutation ACK
is reported as uncertain, not as proof that nothing started. Read-only sessions
cannot be mutated. The tool call and ordinary command receipts retain provenance.

A refused or failed call is a failed tool call. Every Coordinator, Global Router
and Watchdog tool returns its refusal as JSON (`error`, `code`,
`execution_started`, `recovery`) through `coordinator_tools.failed_result`,
which attaches the failed execution verdict: the round settles `error`, and the
transcript (`get_conversation`), the tool card and the model's own view
(`status: error`) all show the failure. Problem: the tool pipeline reads the
verdict only from host execution metadata (`tool_result_failure_verdict`),
never from payload JSON, so these refusals settled `done` and a refused
`restart_server` read as a restart (ROUTER.md R9). Alternatives rejected:
inferring failure from an `error` member in the pipeline (payload JSON is
application data, and successful results carry nested `error` fields of
settled Workers); raising to the executor (its generic `tool_execution_failed`
envelope would drop the typed `code`, `execution_started` and recovery).
Specification: `tests/router/test_owner_delegation.py` runs refused
`restart_server` and delegated Continue/Stop/set_model through the real tool
pipeline.

Alternatives rejected: generating a new user message would change the meaning of
Continue; starting a private executor would split lifecycle authority; granting
an arbitrary synthetic user the owner's identity would bypass dispatch grants.
A Coordinator's next instruction to an ended Worker is the separate `follow_up`
action ("Continuing an ended Worker session" below), never a resume.
Session settings, new messages, human confirmations and deletion remain separate
commands. Verification: `tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_session_control.py`
and `tests/conversation/test_coordinator_session_control.py`.

The browser's own Continue, checkpoint resume, regenerate and guidance answer
follow the same rule for a Worker Turn. When the Turn's current attempt was
dispatched, the conversation command service resumes it through that attempt's
`TrustedDispatch` admission (same task identity, grant fence and assignment
association). When the grant can no longer authorize a resume, the command
refuses with `worker_binding_refused` and an explanation, before anything is
written. Problem: on 2026-11-06 a browser Continue on a Worker conversation
started an ordinary chat attempt in the API host. It had another task identity
and no grant, and its result never reached the Coordinator. Alternative
rejected: refusing every browser Continue on Worker conversations would take
the owner's direct recovery control away for no gain; the binding is the same
authority the Coordinator uses. Turns the owner typed into a Worker
conversation keep their ordinary execution. Verification:
`tests/conversation/test_worker_ui_continue_binding.py`.

A successor Attempt of a Worker Turn resolves its grant through one owner,
`admission.turn_dispatch`. The public attempt command performs the same lookup
before capturing configuration, so a browser resume keeps the assignment's
principal and account. An invalid grant raises `worker_binding_refused` before
acceptance; it never falls back to an unbound owner task. The real-start suite
also verifies that refusal leaves the current attempt and recorded usage intact.
Synthetic callers keep their supplied principal and must already match the
explicit grant; they cannot borrow this owner-originated resume authority.
Problem (2026-11-07 10:45, worker-80e6d733, assignment 6ddab2df): a browser
Continue that ran unbound (before the browser path bound Worker Turns) became
the Turn's current attempt. Both `control_conversation` and the browser read the grant only from the
selected attempt's config, so the Coordinator's continue started, but also
unbound. It ran under the owner's principal with no ledger, and the assignment
kept naming its failed attempt. Decision: when the selected attempt carries no
grant, the dispatch birth's `project-dispatch:<assignment>:output` presentation
locates the assignment. Its grant applies only when the association names this
exact Turn of this recipient conversation; the successor then advances the
association from the attempt it names, and `bind_account` accepts the unbound
attempt as the settled predecessor. A Turn without that presentation reads
nothing more. Alternatives rejected:
- Adopting the unbound attempt's result: its effects ran outside the grant and
  were never recorded, and its checkout may be another one.
- Refusing Continue on such Turns: the lane can then only be re-delegated.
- The latest assignment of the conversation without the exact Turn match: a Turn
  the owner typed into a Worker conversation would be captured by the grant.
Verification:
`tests/conversation/test_worker_attempt_real_start.py::test_a_continue_after_an_unbound_owner_attempt_runs_under_the_assignment`
(Coordinator and owner) and `::test_an_owner_continue_of_a_failed_worker_turn_runs_under_the_assignment`.

#### Continuing an ended Worker session (follow_up)

Problem: one Worker session per goal is faster and cheaper (owner direction
2026-11-08: "adding an elephant could always be discussed within a single
session"), yet a Coordinator could only regenerate an ended Worker Turn or
delegate a new Worker that rediscovered the context. Nothing created a new Turn
in a Worker conversation under its grant, and a new Turn's attempt could not bind
the assignment's account: `bind_account` recognised only the storage-issued
`_resumeSource` of an attempt in the same Turn and refused the rest with "Worker
tool reservations cannot be replenished".

Decision: `control_conversation(action=follow_up, instruction=...)` pins the
Worker's latest settled Turn, its Attempt and its projectionRevision, like every
session control. The instruction becomes that conversation's next Turn: a
`virtual_user` input stamped by the Coordinator (never a human message), and the
ended Turn keeps its attempt, revision and result.
- An unreviewed result whose delivery receipt is not `delivered` continues its own
  assignment (`ProjectDispatchService.follow_up`). The input attaches right after
  the pinned Turn (`requireParentIsLaneTail`), and `TrustedDispatch(follows=...)`
  advances the association only from the exact association the Coordinator read.
  Grant, checkout and account carry over: the accepted config carries the
  predecessor as `_projectDispatchFollowUp`, which `bind_account` checks against
  the settled attempt before it continues the ledgers. The result is reviewed with
  `review_project_work` as usual. As for every in-place resume, the follow-up's start
  reopens the held receipt for its own attempt and its settlement captures the checkout
  ("Delivery follows the attempt that completes" in
  [project integration](modules/project_integration.md)), so the accepted checkpoint
  carries the earlier and the follow-up edits.
- A review is immutable (except for the superseding rework below) and a `delivered`
  receipt never reopens (its checkout may already be reclaimed). Such a result
  continues as a new assignment of the current request in the same conversation,
  through the ordinary delegation with the conversation reused, with a fresh checkout
  of the current branch. Its identity is the pinned association, so a retry replays
  it and another instruction for the same result is refused. An accepted result whose
  delivery has not landed is refused until it lands, except when it never will by
  itself: a conflict holds it (`delivery.conflict`), or a rework superseded its
  accept. Then the successor's brief (`successor_brief(held_checkpoint=...)`) first
  merges the pinned checkpoint into the fresh checkout, and once that lands the held
  delivery settles as delivered by itself (`checkpoint_contained`); the receipt names
  `resolves_held_conflict`. A successor of a rework result links to it
  (`rework_of`, so the graph's `reworkOf` and the repaired work's acceptance checks
  carry over). Lead audit 2026-11-18 #6 (T6, P2): `follow_up` was refused 5 times
  with "Its accepted result has not been delivered yet", and 20 of 86 KL/PL
  delegations existed only to land already-accepted results.

#### A rework supersedes an undelivered review

Problem: a review is immutable, so a lead that accepted a result whose delivery
then conflicted, or that reconsidered an accept or reject, could neither record a
rework nor link a repair to it (`rework_of` requires a recorded rework); every such
case became a new, unlinked repair assignment (lead audit 2026-11-18 D3: 25 TL and
20 KL+PL repair delegations). Decision: `review_project_work(decision=rework)` of
the same `result_revision` replaces a recorded accept or reject whose result was not
delivered; the review keeps the superseded judgment as `review.supersedes`
(`assignment.review.supersedes` in the contract), so history stays append-only.
Repeating it returns the recorded review; anything else stays immutable. A rework
stops a pinned publication: `validate_publication` admits only an accept, and the
`publication` pin stays as evidence. The repair then links to it with `follow_up`
(above) or `delegate_project_work(rework_of=...)`. A delivered result keeps its
review: its work is on the branch, so a repair is new work. Alternatives rejected:
deleting or rewriting the old review (loses the judgment an audit reads);
superseding delivered results (the landed commit would contradict the review);
any decision superseding any other (an accept after a rework needs a new result,
which follow_up produces).
Spec: `test_coordinator_workers.py::test_a_rework_supersedes_an_undelivered_review_and_links_its_repair`.

A replay with identical arguments reports the accepted Turn and starts nothing; once
the session moved on, an older pin is refused. Human messages and steers in the
Worker conversation keep their ordinary paths: a queued human input supersedes the
follow-up, and a running Worker takes context through `send_project_message`, not
`follow_up`. Workers gain no authority. Any Turn of the Coordinator, an automatic
continuation included, follows up the Workers its binding dispatched
(`worker_control(session=True)`), and the owner's delegation (Router R9) never follows up.

#### Attachments on lead messages

Problem: a project lead (Coordinator, Project Router, Global Router) could send
a Worker only text, while a human attaches files of any type to a message. A lead
had to paste paths the Worker might not be able to open from its isolated
checkout, and images never reached the Worker's model as images.

Decision: `delegate_project_work`, `control_conversation(action=follow_up)` and
`send_project_message` take an optional `attachments` list (the shape is
`leadAttachmentReferences` in `contracts/project_dispatch_v1.schema.json`, mirrored
in `contracts/project_messages_v1.json`): file paths, `att_media_<id>` references or
`/api/images/<file>` images. `lib/conversations/lead_attachments.py` is the one
authorization boundary, default deny: a path must be a regular file inside the
sending task's verified read roots, read without following symlinks
(`lib.artifacts.file_snapshot`); a lead (the Global Router or a Project Router,
`lead_attachments.lead_scope`) also reads every registered project root and, as
`worker:<assignment_id>/<path>`, the registered checkout of a Worker its binding
dispatched (ROUTER.md R9, "Leads reach the owner directly"), and no file carrying a
credential is admitted (`lib/conversations/attachment_secrets.py`); an `att_media_` reference must be one of the
owner's own attachments (owner-scoped lookup); an image must be shown in the
sender's conversation: in one of its messages, or in an owner message steered
into the Turn the sender is running. A steer never enters the task's messages,
so its images are read from its records (this executor's delivered ones and
those committed to the live Turn), and only a record whose committed author is
the owner's own input here counts (`require_human_turn`): nonhuman steers and
Global Router forwards add none (2026-11-17: a Coordinator could not forward the
screenshot the owner steered into its turn). The entire batch is checked before
any new file is stored or existing attachment is promoted. File and image bytes
are staged temporarily so retention uses the bytes that passed the checks, not
another read of the source path. An invalid reference refuses the whole batch
(`attachment_refused`). For `delegate_project_work`, the in-memory gate and
existing durable source, request-policy and command checks run before attachment
retention; Worker birth and the grant CAS still have their own authority checks.
Retention is not atomic with those checks or with other retention writes: a
later race or storage failure can leave an already-retained file without a send.
An admitted file is stored once, by digest,
through `lib.media_attachments.ingest_received_file` (origin `agent_message`), the
same retained attachment a channel file or chat upload becomes; no size or count
limit is added. The dispatch request (`request.attachments`) and the mailbox
original (`messageView.attachments`) keep only `{attachmentId, name, mimeType}`.
Delivery:
- a delegation brief or follow-up becomes the Worker's input Turn with canonical
  `projection.attachments`, so model projection and the conversation UI treat
  them exactly as a human's: images viewable, documents readable with
  `read_files(att_media_…)`, any other type named by its stored original path;
- a project message reaches the running model in its inbox carrier with the same
  attachment projection blocks before its text, and each `<project-message>` lists
  its `<attachment attachment_ref=… name=… mime_type=…/>`.

An attachment is context only: an agent-authored message grants no human
authority, and attached files grant none either. Review `rework` creates its repair
through `delegate_project_work`, so repair briefs carry attachments the same way.

Alternatives rejected:
- Copying files into the Worker checkout ties delivery to one workspace and
  bypasses the attachment owner that model projection and the UI already read.
- A second lead-only attachment store would duplicate retention, ownership and
  rendering of the media attachment authority.
- Accepting any absolute path would let a lead exfiltrate files outside what it
  may read.

Verification: `tests/project_mod/test_lead_attachments.py` (real Sidecar: image,
PDF, text and arbitrary binary delivery; refused outside, traversal, symlink,
directory, missing, foreign-owner and unseen-image references; an owner-steered
image admitted, a nonhuman, agent-authored or forwarded steer's refused) and
`tests/project_mod/test_lead_attachment_delivery.py` (each message path and the
contract mirror).

Reuse or split: reuse the session for follow-ups of the same goal, including fixes
the Coordinator would otherwise request with `decision=rework` (follow up before
reviewing). Split into a new Worker for independent parallel work, an unrelated
goal, or a session that keeps failing or whose context no longer helps.

Alternatives rejected:
- Regenerating the Turn discards the result and its context.
- A project message that wakes an idle Worker would make the passive mailbox an
  execution scheduler.
- A new assignment for every follow-up moves the checkout and the account on each
  step and loses undelivered work.
- Reopening a reviewed assignment would make reviews mutable.

Verification: `tests/conversation/test_worker_attempt_real_start.py::test_a_follow_up_continues_an_ended_worker_session_as_its_next_turn`
(through review and a committed delivery of both attempts' edits),
`::test_a_follow_up_after_a_final_result_continues_the_session_as_a_linked_assignment` and
`::test_a_follow_up_is_refused_before_anything_starts_when_the_session_cannot_continue`
(SQL and native), and the follow-up specifications in
`tests/project_mod/test_coordinator_session_control.py` and `test_coordinator_workers.py`.

Problem: permitted Coordinator tools were absent from default keyword-routed
wire schemas and had to be rediscovered on every new task. Decision:
`coordinator_policy.prepare_coordinator_tool_surface` derives required-direct
exposure from the existing role policy intersected with the **post-gate**
executable catalog, limited to the role's usage-derived core: `ROUTER_DIRECT_TOOLS`
for the Global Router, `COORDINATOR_DIRECT_TOOLS` for a Project Coordinator, every
Watchdog tool. The role's other tools stay eager, so only a route's tool-name budget
hides them ([LLM_COST_OPTIMIZATION.md](LLM_COST_OPTIMIZATION.md#6-gateway-tool-name-budget)).
Assembly applies it to genuine bound roots and validated
continuations, not ordinary chats, foreign owners or transient children. It
preserves Plan/readonly/selection/authorization checks and does not expand a
worker's issued tool scope. Alternatives rejected: making tools global `_ALWAYS`
entries grants irrelevant visibility; a separate role whitelist drifts from
execution authority. `test_coordinator_tool_surface.py` verifies actual wire
schemas, retries, discovery modes and negative authority cases;
`test_coordinator_tool_name_budget.py` keeps every role surface within the Claude
tool-name budget.

Problem: assignment inventory, conversation inventory and actual execution were
conflated, hiding zero-Turn workers. Decision: persisted empty catalog entries
remain navigable; transient local drafts retain their existing cleanup. Search
hits never stand in for full metadata: exact-ID navigation hydrates canonical
owned metadata before activation and does not move the global paging boundary.
Prefixed coordinator/worker IDs participate in instant local ID search.

`GET /api/v4/project/coordination/conversations` supplies the passive roster from
`coordinator_conversations.conversation_page`. One call reads at most 64 metadata
headers through the selected authority, filters owner/project/archive scope,
and returns a scope-bound keyset cursor and explicit coverage. Empty matching
pages may still have `nextCursor`; they are not evidence of no workers. No
transcripts, new worker store, grants, model calls or execution are created.
Each row also carries the stored `folderId` (null when unfiled) from the same
catalog header. Problem: the roster lists every conversation of the project,
owner chats included, and the sidebar adopts rows its catalog lacks as catalog
shells; without the folder those shells projected as uncategorized although
storage filed them. That happened whenever the global sidebar catalog had not
loaded the row, past its 500-row window or while that read failed. Decision:
the row carries the stored membership and adoption applies it through the
ordinary catalog merge, whose freshness guard keeps a newer local move.
Alternatives rejected: adopting only group members still leaves Worker shells
folderless and miscounted; deriving a folder from the project path invents
membership storage does not hold, because folders may share project roots.
Problem: this bounded roster read unconditionally walked all assignment history,
even for an empty/nonmatching page or conversations with creation provenance.
Decision: first apply visibility/project filters and use explicit creation source;
load the existing owned, paged history once only if a returned row needs backfill.
Legacy backfill still visits every history page in its existing order, and an
authority failure still propagates when that evidence is required. Truthy but
invalid explicit sources still project as absent. Alternatives rejected: a
cache adds freshness/identity state, and
stopping after a recent assignment could replace the original provenance.
The inventory specifications cover unavailable-but-unneeded history, mixed
explicit/legacy rows and cursor coverage. `benchmarks/owned_inventory_reads.py`
counts history method calls with synthetic ports, not actual storage RPCs.
With 64 explicit-source rows and 2,000/10,000 historical assignments, three
alternating fresh-process pairs retained identical response digests while
history method calls fell from 63/313 to zero; the metadata page remained one.
Median per-process warm CPU medians were 0.494→0.113 ms and 1.963→0.111 ms.
Actual authority validation, transport and HTTP encoding are excluded.
The UI replaces each roster page on demand, joins assignment recipients, and
never silently truncates a lifetime inventory. A zero-Turn shell without an
assignment is **Not started**, never queued. Other history remains distinct.

Every coordinator/work row exposes a copyable reference and inspectable IDs
without Debug mode. **Ask assistant to diagnose** prepares an ordinary visible
read-only-project draft containing the exact conversation/project references.
It never auto-sends or overwrites existing text/attachments. Normal conversation
navigation and Turn rendering remain the sole owners.

Context organization and a fresh working view are distinct from coordinator
replacement. Organizing (compacting) a coordinator or coordinated-project
conversation schedules a working-view compaction that its next model call
applies; canonical Turns used by authorization/report references are never
folded, and the persistence adapter still rejects the destructive rewrite at
canonical application. See `CONTEXT_COMPACTION.md#coordinator-context-lifecycle`
for conservative project protection and the remaining historical-reference
limitation. No safe fresh-view (reset) command exists: the management surface
says so and points organizing to the conversation's context meter rather than
calling `/create` as a fallback. **Replace
Coordinator** lives under management, explains preserved history and invalidated
old authority, requires confirmation, then rereads binding before CAS. Read
failure has a read-only retry, not an automatic repeat of replacement.

### Fresh Coordinator backend guarantee

Problem: selecting a historical chat imports unrelated conversation context, and
revision-derived candidate IDs could cause creation to adopt a pre-existing chat.
Decision: `/create` at the current binding revision creates a new ordinary empty
conversation with a fresh identity and only canonical project/folder settings,
then CAS-binds it. It never scans or copies historical chats, transcripts or
settings. A pre-existing identity or failed create fails closed, never becoming
proof of ownership of the candidate. Creation starts no execution.

An explicit current-revision create can replace an already available Coordinator
without deleting or altering the old conversation. GET is read-only. Existing
valid bindings remain usable on restart; stale create retries return the current
available binding without allocating another chat. Stale unavailable bindings
conflict. `/bind` now only clears a binding or reaffirms its current available
ID; selecting another existing chat returns 400. Clearing uses revision CAS.
Existing clients offering historical selection must use explicit creation instead.

Alternatives rejected: automatic current/latest-chat binding contaminates context;
checking that a historical chat is empty still adopts user-owned history; predictable
candidate reuse can adopt an orphan that gained content. Creation and binding
remain separate storage commands: each attempted create allocates at most one
empty conversation, and a failed binding CAS can leave that chat unbound. Retries
never adopt it or delete user data; concurrent requests cannot replace a newer
binding because CAS retains authority. No background loop or cleanup worker is
added. Narrow guards: `tests/project_mod/test_project_coordination.py` and
`tests/api/test_api_v1_project_coordination.py`.

The demand-loaded workspace uses the project's native dialog and shared tabs:
Tasks, Conversations and Advanced. Tasks separates active work, attention-needed
work and completed results, with a direct coordinator conversation as the primary
action. Completed filtering follows the existing admission-ordered history cursors;
it can read an older page, but never calls a page-scoped count a lifetime total.
Review/dependency/delivery state and exact references stay in the task's result
and details disclosure, never as a human to-do count. Completion alone never
means delivered. Conversations open the ordinary conversation canvas, including
conversations with no work record. The same project entrance returns users to
the Coordinator. Advanced retains permissions, usage, files, messages and
coordinator replacement behind explicit disclosures, with no disabled placeholder
actions. Temporary child interaction remains in the existing Swarm/agent surfaces.
Management forms and file/message reads mount only when disclosed. The coordinator
binding paints the shell before task evidence settles; roster and usage reads never
block the initial task view. Committed project events refresh the open overview;
demand accumulated on another tab is read on return without remounting forms or
message pagination. Only one workspace read runs at a time; events during that
read coalesce into one follow-up. Unchanged rendered facts retain their DOM;
changed cards restore focus by work/assignment ID.
Background failures keep the workspace usable with a visible retry. Closing
disposes the subscription and pending refresh timer. A failed board or assignment
read is shown as unavailable, not an empty project. The shared graph projection
in `project-coordination-graph.ts` renders task nodes linked to their ordinary
worker conversations. Recorded assignment dependencies and rework links are
explicit edges; dispatch links are visually distinct, and layout never implies
a timeline. A separate Coordinator node dispatches and tracks; its own work is
excluded from worker execution progress.

Assignments are the primary graph evidence when present. Otherwise the graph
shows observed work with an explicit coverage caveat: Board work IDs and assignment
IDs have no shared public task identity, so they must not be summed or guessed to
be duplicates. Queued/running/completed/failed/cancelled/blocked/unknown remain
distinct. Unknown prerequisites and missing references remain visible. Progress
counts completed tasks against displayed tasks, not a fabricated whole-project
percentage; unavailable progress is never 0%. Delivery requires both canonical
receipt confirmation and delivered status. Assignment-only status changes
invalidate the graph even when observed work stays unchanged.
The setup action creates a new empty Coordinator instead of selecting an old chat;
previous conversations are preserved.

Workers now use `send_agent_message` / `get_agent_messages` for exact-run team
communication. Original server-stamped identity and the live child assignment
fence sends; worker-to-worker messages and interim reports to a running root
reuse the same durable run mailbox and verified-input receipts. The root target
is `agent_id == swarm_run_id`. A worker cannot activate retained agents, change
permissions or wake an idle root. See [team identities](RETAINED_AGENTS.md).

Alternatives rejected: a second supervisor engine duplicates cancellation and
budget authority; independent worker chat widgets duplicate Turn rendering;
putting all operational forms before status makes ordinary coordination harder.
Trailing-edge debounce can starve updates under continuous events; overlapping
reads waste work and full background rerenders discard user input and focus.

Resource impact: no new idle executor or poller. A bound root pays one extra
binding/owned-conversation lookup per context assembly and at most 600 role
prompt tokens. The open overview reads binding, board and existing assignments;
project pushes coalesce in a fixed 300 ms window. The slow-read regression sends
10 events across one second: one refresh starts at 300 ms, concurrency stays one,
and all events during it produce one follow-up. No timer polls while idle or on
another tab. Board input groups cap records at 100 each. Team/roster uses explicit
page navigation instead of a silent 100-entry slice; visible lists render eight
rows at a time. An opened workspace requests one bounded roster page alongside
existing reads; no roster reads occur merely to display the fixed sidebar entry.
Assignment history loads one page of at most 32 owned records;
the graph retains a defensive 128-node input ceiling for other callers.
For 128 assignments with up to eight prerequisites each, the default work-first
view measured 146 DOM elements and zero mounted graph nodes. Explicitly opening
topology renders 128 assignments plus the Coordinator: Chromium measured 4,156
DOM elements and about 55 ms for the combined initial render/expansion/layout,
with a 480 px viewport. The browser gate bounds the default view below 400
elements and the expanded view below 5,000. Only one responsive wire layer is
visible; no observer or timer is allocated.
This is a local layout measurement, not a universal latency promise.
Browser tests cover 1440/390/320 widths and all three themes; unit/pipeline tests
cover team isolation, sender-key collisions, no-wake, recovery and input receipts.

Problem: the old assignment endpoint returned all evidence while the browser
silently sliced the first 128 records, hiding new work as history grew.
Decision: `history.py` reads immutable admission order through a bounded semantic
range and point documents. GET `/api/v4/project/coordination/assignments` returns
`historyPage`: newest first, at most 32 facts, and owned `olderCursor` / `newerCursor`.
An exact `commandId` or `assignmentId` lookup is independent of history pages;
these selectors cannot be combined with a cursor. Paging is passive, preserves
legacy cursors through cutover, and never extends a grant or acknowledges a report.
A page is one index range plus point documents: each document is consistent by
itself, and listed index rows and sequences never change, so a birth committing
during the build (the only commit that moves the head) never makes the page read
again. Until 2026-11-13 the page required its load to start on the head its index
list saw, so under a birth storm it read itself again up to four times and then
refused ("Concurrent history changes").
Read-only history omits unrelated source account/occurrence documents. Execution
content in a page is a 2,000-character preview; the ordinary Worker conversation
and exact lookup retain the complete evidence. Tenant filtering precedes exposure;
no project-total count is inferred from one page.

Problem: history deep-copied each complete execution projection before throwing
away everything except that existing preview. Decision: only the history-page
view selects the content preview before copying; exact assignment/command reads
still return complete detached evidence, and settlement still reads metadata.
Alternatives rejected: truncating the detail view loses evidence, and caching
history can hide current execution or authority changes. Binding and tenant
checks remain on every read. `test_project_dispatch_history_projection.py`
guards omitted detail traversal, complete exact reads and unchanged settlement.
An eight-worker fixture with a 1,879,310-byte projection per Turn measured
118.14/118.16 ms before and 0.041/0.041 ms after in isolated ABBA medians. This
measures Python view projection only; native reads and HTTP schema work remain
outside this fixture and still contribute to the endpoint's latency.

Problem: every page refresh still read each bound Worker Turn with its complete
projection to display that 2,000-character preview. On the older live generation a
32-row page took about 1.2 s, and most refreshes answered 304 only after the
complete build. Decision: a
page reads only Turn metadata (`include_projection=False`) for a Turn it showed in
the previous build of the same owner/project. It reuses that preview only when
`projectionRevision` and public status both match: the revision fences the stored
projection, and status selects live or settled read normalization. Any other or
changed Turn reads its body once. Attempt status, association identity, grant and
tenant filtering, delivery receipts and dependency evidence stay live on every
build, and exact lookups still return complete evidence. The route passes the
process `HistoryPreviews`. Each build replaces its owner/project slot with the
previews it displayed, so retention is one contract page per slot, with no size,
age or TTL knob. A Turn read for its preview asks the authority for
`content_preview_chars` (2,000) on a metadata read and receives `contentPreview`
instead of the projection: running Workers change revision every round, and on
2026-11-15 concurrent builds that decoded their multi-MB projections drove the
API host to 1.8 GiB (`docs/RESOURCE_CONTROL.md`, "Assignment history
previews"). An earlier version of this decision rejected that read flag because
warm builds of settled Turns did not need it. Caching whole views or validated rows would hide live execution or
authority changes (see the validation decision below). Keying on the revision alone
misses the native execution-epoch normalization, which can change public status
without a new revision. The duplicate canvas and sidebar reads are joined in the
browser instead (see the same-task read join under the conversation-first
project entry). `benchmarks/project_assignment_page_reads.py` measured 32
bound rows with 2.1 MB settled projections (4.2 MB public Turns), 16 of them with a
file profile. Unchanged builds made 116 storage RPCs in both arms. Warm builds returned
88,569 instead of 134,919,109 response bytes, reading 32 Turn metadata records
instead of 32 bodies. One changed Turn added exactly one body read (117 RPCs).
Two alternating fresh-process pairs (12 warm builds per arm) measured warm medians
of 788 to 134 ms on native and 462 to 79 ms on SQLite; these are host observations.
The first build after process start is unchanged. The remaining
native cost is mostly metadata reads (about 2.5 ms each for a 4 MB document,
because the native metadata read still materializes the stored document) plus the
validated page load (about 33 ms). Guards:
`test_project_dispatch_history_projection.py` covers reuse, revision and status
changes, retention and binding fences;
`test_project_dispatch_history.py::test_page_reads_a_worker_turn_body_only_when_its_revision_changes`
repeats reuse and a real revision change on SQLite and native.

Problem: every `historyPage` row returned the complete request. In a live 32-row
page of 660,536 bytes, `request.delegation` alone was 361,664 bytes and
`request.profile` 159,475, yet no list consumer reads the delegation, source,
model, budget or wake fields. Decision (owner-authorized contract change): a page
row is the `historyRow` of `contracts/project_dispatch_v1.schema.json`, the `view`
whose request is reduced to `historyRequest`: the request fields that the canvas,
sidebar, dispatch drawer, rework draft, dependency picker and scope-change targets
read. Source, model, budget, delegation and wake fields are omitted. Exact
`commandId`/`assignmentId` lookups return `assignmentLookup`, which holds complete
views, so reconciliation still compares the whole grant. The service derives
`HISTORY_REQUEST_FIELDS` from the contract. Tenant filtering, principal checks,
cursors, passive reads and `HistoryPreviews` are unchanged, and
`get_project_workers` still reads complete repository rows. The browser types
page rows as the generated `ProjectDispatchHistoryRow`, so `tsc` rejects any list
read of an omitted field. Alternatives rejected: dropping `profile.tools`
(157,461 live bytes of project-mode tool lists), because the rework draft restores
the profile from the row and would need an exact read per click; a separate lean
endpoint or query flag, which adds a second list contract; and trimming in the
browser after transfer. Measured: the captured live page projects offline to
286,467 bytes (56.6% smaller) and validates as the new `historyPage`.
`benchmarks/project_assignment_page_reads.py` (32 rows, SQLite) returns 81,479
instead of 87,303 page bytes with unchanged storage work (116 RPCs and 88,569 warm
response bytes in both trees). Guards: `test_project_dispatch_history_projection.py`
(projection, complete exact lookups, contract mirror and generated DTO drift),
`test_coordinator_workers.py` (a delegated Worker row omits `delegation`; its exact
lookup keeps it), the `project-dispatch.test.ts` parity spec (rows render and
draft rework identically without the omitted fields) and
`test_project_coordinator_browser.py::test_manage_work_reads_server_history_rows`
(real server rows in Chromium: the rework draft restores `profile.tools`, completed
rows are dependency pins and every current grant is a scope-change target).

Problem: a history read validated each complete record/assignment and then
repeated the same nested request and model-attempt schema walks. Decision:
the canonical record/assignment schema is the single structural validation pass;
ownership, profile, derived admission, occurrence uniqueness, budgets and graph
checks still run on every read. No authority is cached; only the structural verdict
of exact unchanged stored content is reused (see "Stored-row schema verdicts" below).
Alternatives rejected: skipping whole-record validation loses durable integrity;
caching permission verdicts needs an invalidation protocol.
The complete in-memory history-page benchmark
`benchmarks/project_dispatch_history_validation.py` retains 28 assignments with
100 model occurrences each. Fresh-process ABBA medians were 699.1/705.6 ms before
and 379.1/365.3 ms after; CPU was 695.5/702.8 ms and 377.2/363.5 ms. Every page
matched the independently constructed oracle and every backing record remained
unchanged. These are Python history-work measurements, excluding native I/O and
browser transport. Nested corruption/ownership guards cover both storage formats
in `test_project_dispatch_record_validation.py`.

Problem: a page row with dependencies reloaded the dispatch snapshot its page had
just loaded (head, row, prerequisite, head again: four RPCs for one prerequisite)
before reading its live evidence. Decision: `history.page` returns its loaded value
as `snapshot`, which holds every row's reachable prerequisites, and the service
evaluates the dependency states of that build's rows against it. A row's states now
come from the same snapshot as the row itself; Stop, binding, conversation, source,
attempt, Turn and workspace evidence stay live reads of every build, and nothing
outlives the build. Dispatch admission keeps its own selection and reload.
Alternatives rejected: caching dependency views or prerequisite evidence across
builds (owner decision: no cross-request caches; they would hide revocation and
Stop), and keeping the per-row reload for a fresher grant (it can disagree with the
grant the same row displays, and the next build reads both again).
`benchmarks/project_assignment_page_reads.py --dependencies` (32 rows, 16 with a
prerequisite, SQLite) made 180 instead of 244 storage RPCs per warm build, 35
instead of 99 of them dispatch document reads, with oracle-equal pages; fresh-process
ABBA warm medians were 164.3/166.2 ms before and 105.9/102.3 ms after (host
observations). Guards: `test_project_dispatch_dependencies.py` (page states equal the
former per-row reload, one dispatch record read per build) and
`test_project_dispatch_history.py::test_page_dependency_rows_add_no_snapshot_reads`
(SQLite, PostgreSQL and native: a build's dispatch RPCs equal its page load's).

Problem: each 32-row page still made about 115 storage round trips (an assignment
document, a Turn, an attempt and a delivery workspace per row), and in a busy
process every round trip waits several times to be scheduled. The old repo's
COORD-PAGE-READS probe measured page p50 11-18 s and p95 142-155 s on the N=1000
synthetic Coordinator load, with the page thread's CPU 0.2% of its wall time.
Decision: a build reads each kind of evidence for all its rows in one exchange
(`lib.storage.port.query_many`, see
[STORAGE.md](STORAGE.md#commands-queries-and-idempotency)): the selected
documents (`SegmentedDispatch.get_many`, used by every point selection), then
the bound Turns (metadata where a remembered preview may still hold, the
server-cut content preview otherwise), the attempts, the previews whose Turn
metadata changed, and the delivery workspaces (`service._HistoryEvidence`).
Each read is the same live read as before and is consumed where the row read it,
so failures surface on the same row; each distinct project path resolves once
per build. A page of 32 rows now costs the round trips of a page of one: 7, plus
one when Turns changed. Injected single-read ports keep the row-by-row path.
Alternatives rejected: an exact cache of settled rows' views (Turn, attempt and
workspace evidence can change after settlement, so exactness needs a live
witness per row, which is the same reads; the slowest pages are in busy phases
with live rows; and the no-cross-request-cache decision above stands), a
projection maintained at settlement (every Turn, attempt and delivery writer in
other owners would have to maintain it), and per-domain multi-get operations
(four catalog operations on both authorities and a native engine rebuild and
redeployment for the same round trips). Guards:
`test_project_dispatch_history.py::test_page_round_trips_do_not_grow_with_its_rows`
(115 round trips before) and `::test_page_content_equals_the_row_by_row_reads`
(SQLite and native). Re-measured here with `benchmarks/project_assignment_page_reads.py
--rows 32 --builds 3` on an exported pre-change tree and this one (same engine
built from this checkout, `TOFU_TEST_TOFUDB_BIN`): SQLite 115 → 7 round trips
per 32-row build (116 → 8 when one Turn changed), native 115 → 7 (116 → 8),
with identical RPC counts and oracle-equal pages on both. What remains is per
read, not per round trip: the old repo's VM runs put the residual per-read cost
at ~155 ms while 300 Workers are born and ~60 ms once 1000 run, the wait of a
host process whose threads saturate the interpreter lock.

Problem: when a client disconnects, Quart cancels only the coroutine awaiting the
view. The former sync view's worker thread kept building the whole page (every
Turn, attempt, delivery and dependency read) for nobody, while polling clients
frequently gave up on slow builds; the retained application logs hold hundreds of
SLOW/HEAVY warnings for this GET. Decision: GET assignments is an async view that
runs the unchanged build on a worker thread, sets an event when its coroutine is
cancelled and joins the worker before re-raising the cancellation.
`ProjectDispatchService.history_page` checks that predicate before the page load,
before each evidence exchange and between rows and raises `HistoryBuildCancelled`;
the exchange or row whose reads are in flight finishes and nothing later is read.
A cancelled build sends no response and
keeps the previous previews slot. Exact command/assignment lookups are single reads
and do not check it. Alternatives rejected: a build deadline (a tuning knob that also
cuts slow legitimate builds), abandoning the thread as before (its reads continue),
and checking between the reads of one row (a row's evidence belongs together and
one row bounds the remainder). Through Quart's ASGI test client, a 32-row page
disconnected during its first row: the former view read 63 more records after the
disconnect (all 32 rows) and ended without its thread; the async view read one more
record (the in-flight row's attempt) and ended 0.4 ms after that read returned.
Guards: `test_project_dispatch_routes.py::test_client_disconnect_stops_the_history_build_before_its_next_row`
and the cancelled-build specs in `test_project_dispatch_history_projection.py`.

The conversation canvas, overview and grant controls replace the current page
when navigating; they do not accumulate old graphs or a cursor stack. Older
pages expose completed nodes, and Back to latest returns to current work. Project
switches reset the cursor and fence late responses. Captions scope statistics
to the page. The existing 128-target scope-change control gathers bounded pages
before offering an all-project Stop: more than 128 eligible pins, an incomplete
scan (at most 128 pages), or a failed read cannot authorize a partial Stop.
Per-task revoke remains available through history. The server still rechecks
the exact complete target set at mutation. Alternatives rejected: raising a silent
slice, retaining every page in the DOM, offset cursors that shift on new work,
and deriving all-project authority from a single page.

Guards: `test_project_dispatch_history.py` on SQLite/PostgreSQL/native,
`test_project_dispatch_segments.py` for reverse range ownership/exclusivity,
the real route/auth tests, and `project-coordination-surface.test.ts` for 160
reachable tasks with 32 retained task nodes and stale-project response fences.
Chromium exercises five history pages, both directions, latest and ordinary
Worker navigation at 1440/390/320 in all three themes without provider calls.

## Passive project conversation messages

Problem: `_peerNoWake` guards only some legacy queue drains; restoring its sender
can trigger paid work and default synthetic input to human attribution.
Decision: portable record CAS stores passive originals and consideration without
a dispatchable queue source, resident worker or polling thread. The existing
natural root round acquires optional input after complete tool pairs.
Alternative rejected: heartbeat-only guards cannot protect startup/completion drains.

### Messages wake an idle lead (owner request 2026-11-18)

Problem: a message to a lead waited for that lead's next Turn. The 2026-11-18
lead audit (C1) found 37 of 78 messages to the Global Router arriving while it
ran no attempt; they waited 678 min in total (median 10.5 min, p90 45.1 min), a
Project Router's landing consult 38.7 min, and the Router's attempts covered
19.4% of 57.7 h. Decision: `project_messages.wake_idle_lead` starts one
nonhuman notice Turn (`[Project message wake: …]`, initiator `peer`) in a
current lead (`lead_role`) whose mailbox holds pending messages of eligible
senders; the messages themselves arrive through the ordinary inbox delivery of
that Turn's rounds. It runs after each `send_project_message` (the result's
`wake` names the outcome: `started`, `busy`, `next_step` for a Worker recipient,
`quiet_hours`, `nothing_pending`) and after each lead Turn's settlement drain
(a message that arrived after its last round). Coalescing is structural: the
command id names the newest pending message, so one wake exists per lead and
pending frontier, and the default dispatch mode starts nothing in a busy lane
(`busy`): a running lead reads the message at its next round and its `poll`
ends for it ("Waiting for Workers"), so it is never steered a second notice.
The Global Router is not woken during quiet hours, as its relays are not
(ROUTER.md R10). The wake Turn acts under the owner's delegation to every lead
(ROUTER.md R9). Alternatives rejected: a debounce interval or per-lead wake
budget (a tuning knob; the frontier identity already bounds wakes); steering
the notice into a running Turn (the inbox already delivers there); waking
Workers (a Worker reads messages at its next step and is woken by its lead).
Specifications: `tests/project_mod/test_project_messages.py::test_a_message_wakes_an_idle_lead_once_per_pending_frontier`,
`tests/router/test_cross_project_lead_messages.py`.

Durable history. Problem: one record per original owner/project/recipient held
at most 128 originals for its lifetime and rewrote the whole list on every
receipt, so a months-long Project or Global Router exhausts its mailbox.
Decision: `ProjectMailbox` keeps append-only segmented history on the generic
record operations (key layout: `history` in `contracts/project_messages_v1.json`).
The head holds only the open segment (at most `messagesPerSegment` = 128, the
old per-record bound) and the sealed count. Absorbing into a full open segment
first seals it (segment copy with per-sender links, sender pointers, pending
index) before the head CAS that advances it. Position = segment × 128 + slot
never changes, so `before_id` stays a stable cursor; sealed receipts CAS their
segment record and keep per-message revision CAS. A legacy single record is
read as open segment 0 and sealed by its next absorption; nothing is copied away
or deleted first. Acquisition reads sealed pending originals through the
pending index and skips departed senders without reading their segments.
Alternatives rejected: raising the cap (still a lifetime ceiling, larger
rewrites); archiving or evicting considered originals (durable user evidence);
writing every locator while sealing (measured 1.9 s, up to 21 s under host
load, on each 129th send); `record.list` sender/pending indexes (SQL prefix
lists are unindexed `LIKE` scans of the whole namespace); a new two-authority
storage operation (one head CAS then committed each append atomically).

Appends never touch the head (decision 2026-11-13). Problem: every send was a
CAS of the recipient's one head record with `casAttempts` (4) tries, so reports
that arrive together lose to each other. The synthetic-agent harness
("Coordination at scale" below) lost 37 of 100 Worker reports at N=100 and 238
of 300 at N=300, even after three extra model rounds of retries, and each try
rewrote the whole open segment. Decision: a send is a positional append that is
idempotent by message ID, built from existing record operations:

1. Ticket. The sender writes the mailbox's tickets record without an expected
   version. Both authorities serialize the writes to one record and return
   the new version, which is the ticket: concurrent senders never conflict.
   The command ID is fixed per message ID (and per voided ticket, below), so
   a retried draw replays the same ticket instead of drawing another.
2. Claim. The sender creates its message locator `{ticket}` at version 0.
   This is the history-wide idempotency check: a retry finds the claim and
   delivers under it.
3. Original. The sender creates the immutable original at its ticket's arrival
   record, also at version 0. The send is durable from here on.

The recipient's own acquisition and receipts (`inject`, `confirm`, `consider`)
absorb arrivals in ticket order into the open segment, sealing it as before,
one segment's room per head CAS. So the head has one writer, and positions,
paging, per-sender links, the pending index and receipt revisions keep their
meaning. An arrival counts only at the ticket its locator claims and only once;
any other ticket is voided. A ticket's position is `ticketBase + ticket - 1`,
minus earlier voided tickets; `ticketBase` is the head-era history length the
first ticketed head write fixes. Readers never write: they read arrivals not
yet absorbed after the absorbed history, in ticket order, so a reader before
and after absorption sees the same order. A receipt for an original behind a
stalled ticket is refused as uncertain until a later pass absorbs it.

A ticket whose original is missing stops absorption once (`stalled`). The next
pass that still finds it missing creates a void marker at that arrival, at
version 0. If the sender died between ticket and original, its retry finds
its ticket void, draws a new one and moves its claim. A live sender that
loses that race does the same. Either way every message lands exactly once,
and absorption never waits on a sender.

Head-era history. The head's ticketed form stores the open segment under
`open`, so writers from before this decision find no `messages` and fail
closed. The first ticketed head write gives every head-era original that has no
locator (a legacy single record's) its `{segment}` locator; a ticket claim that
a retry of one of them made first yields to it, and absorption drops that copy.
A head-era claim proven unfilled is replaced by a ticket claim. The one
residual: a retry made before that first write returns its own copy of the
original, with the same ID and body. The tool contract did not change.

Rolling back and forward. A head converted back to the `messages` form for an
earlier version keeps its `tickets` and `voided`: the conversion absorbs every
drawn ticket and leaves no `{ticket}` claim at or below `tickets`, and the
earlier writers keep head fields they do not know. Read again, such a head
resumes them with `ticketBase` = its history length - `tickets` + the voided
count, so the next ticket lands after every original, the earlier writers'
appends included, and no earlier ticket is read or voided again. A head that
predates tickets carries neither and starts at ticket 1.

`ProjectMailbox.convert_back()` performs the conversion, offline, with the
application stopped. It absorbs every drawn ticket (one never written is
voided), points each `{ticket}` claim at its original's segment, and stores the
head last. A claim whose ticket holds no original gets segment 0, which the
earlier writers prove dangling and replace on retry. It finds every claim by
paging the mailbox's locators with `record.list` once, the one listing in this
layout. Converting again, or converting a mailbox that never took a ticket,
writes nothing.

Alternatives rejected:
- an engine-side append operation: a new storage primitive that needs
  certification, and the ticket gives the same conflict-free order with
  existing operations;
- per-sender lanes: the recipient needs a directory of senders, which is a
  contended record again, or `record.list`, an unindexed `LIKE` scan on SQL;
- more CAS tries or backoff: a tuned knob that still rewrites the open segment
  O(N²) times;
- senders sealing segments on their path: that puts every sender pointer
  write of a seal on the 129th send;
- senders reading the head to find a legacy original's retry: it puts an
  open-segment read on every send to cover one legacy case.

A mailbox write that loses its CAS to a record already holding exactly the
value it attempted has succeeded (CAS-CONFLICT-1, 2026-11-13): two passes that
absorb the same arrivals compute identical heads, segment copies, sender
pointers and pending indexes. In the N=210 rounds cells, 203 of 208 conflicts
were such identical writes; the losing pass aborted its seal or lost an
acknowledgement, and messages were re-offered. `_put` re-reads on
`database_conflict` and returns the stored version when the value is equal; any
other conflict, such as a second confirmation whose receipt differs, still
raises and retries.

`reference/consult/report/question/assign/steer` classify context, not execution
grants. Accepted is a stated commitment, not executor birth or delivery.

Delivery accepts (owner request 2026-11-18). Problem: the lead audit (C4, D2,
T5) counted 334 of 335 `consider_project_message` decisions `accepted` (a
ceremony of 321 calls and 211 message-only model rounds, about $59), and 19
refusals `Message revision changed`; all 9 of the tofu Project Router's passed
the revision they read, which input confirmation had already moved from 1 to
2. Decision: input confirmation records `accepted` with `decisionAuto: true`
and `deliveredRevision` (the revision the delivery showed) in the same receipt.
The delivered `<project-message>` block carries `revision="…"`, and a
rejected, deferred or conflict decision passes it as `expected_revision`; that
revision stays valid while only the delivery changed the message, after which
only the current revision decides (revision CAS as before).
`consider_project_message` left the Project Coordinator's direct core
(docs/LLM_COST_OPTIMIZATION.md section 6). Alternatives rejected: dropping the
revision check (a stale decision could overwrite a newer one); not bumping
the revision at confirmation (readers of the page lose the change signal). Causal replies must identify an actual incoming
message from the addressed sender. Queries never read private transcripts.

Owned current metadata is checked before send/acquisition, at logical-call
admission, and at the canonical prepared-input physical-attempt boundary. This is not a persistent conversation
project-generation fence: returning to the original project can make retained
originals eligible again. Different-project mailboxes never inherit those records.
Restored witness carriers are validated even when fresh acquisition fails; child
bootstrap removes previous-run mailbox carriers from its copied checkpoint, not
from durable evidence. The exact same mailbox/run retains confirmed carrier
context during recovery, subject to live dispatch validation. Conversation-mode
children retain parent context text but strip its mailbox receipt authority.
Unknown/mismatched authority fails before dispatch; a new authorized turn remains
the execution owner.

Sender validation has one owner, `project_messages.validate_sender`, shared by
acquisition and logical/physical input admission. Problem: acquisition admitted
the owner's currently bound Router Watchdog across projects, but dispatch
reapplied only the ordinary same-project rule and rejected its messages before
the model call. Decision: all three boundaries use the same live sender rule;
only the current available Watchdog binding and, between two current leads,
another project's lead (below) grant cross-project exceptions. Recipient
ownership/project identity and durable-original checks remain mandatory.
Alternatives rejected: trusting injection-time eligibility would miss
revocation while waiting to send; allowing every cross-project sender would
widen authority. Tests cover all three provider protocols, binding
replacement/unavailability, foreign owners, recipient rebinding and storage
read failures, plus delivery through real storage authorities.

Cross-project lead messages (owner request 2026-11-11, [ROUTER.md R13](ROUTER.md#r13-project-leads-message-each-other-across-projects-owner-request-2026-11-11)).
Problem: only same-project conversations could message each other, so one
project's lead could reach neither another project's lead nor the Global
Router, and the owner repeated goals, context and the resources each project
may use to every lead. Decision: the owner's current leads (the bound
Coordinators of a project and of the router home) send, receive and consider
messages across projects with unchanged bounds, idempotency, kinds and
receipts. `lead_role` is the one live rule: `send_project_message` and an
outgoing `get_project_messages` read require both ends to lead, and
`validate_sender(owner, sender, project, recipient=...)` admits another
project's sender only while it and the recipient both lead, so a rebound lead
keeps its originals as evidence and nothing new crosses. The original records
`senderProject` (also in `ProjectMessageView`) and its model input carries
`sender-role`, `sender-project` and the contract's nonhuman label. Workers,
ordinary chats, subagents and other owners stay out; Workers relay through
their lead. Leads find each other and the owner's `RESOURCES.md` with
`list_project_leads`, a Coordinator tool that Workers are denied
(`workerDeniedTools`) and continuation grants omit; a continuation answers
through `send_project_message`. Alternatives rejected: relaying through the
Global Router (a paid router turn per message and lost reply threads), letting
every conversation cross projects (Workers would commit behind their lead's
back), and per-project allow-lists, quotas or rates (new knobs; owner scope and
the message bounds already bound the traffic). Specification:
`tests/router/test_cross_project_lead_messages.py`.

## Input receipts and recovery limits

Exact carrier text is matched only in input-bearing fields. A request-local
observer composes with the existing model-budget attempt lease, and revalidates
scope against sealed translated input after dispatcher waits and before provider
ingress, including floor resends. This adds no second budget/dispatch owner.
Tool descriptions, routing and metadata cannot prove consumption. Only matches
from the selected verified response become eligible for ACK; failed/dropped input
cannot inherit previous IDs. Unobserved custom transports produce no physical
receipt. This does not independently prove serialized-wire identity after every
possible downstream adaptation. Credential recovery, when present, must wrap
per-attempt receipt validation, never the reverse. Local delivery failures retain
the original cause, not a provider fallback; the shared-runtime composition
specification executes only where those owners are present.

Validation is not atomic with the network send: a revocation immediately after
checking can race ingress. Relay/proxy-internal retries need their own enforcement
and are not claimed here. A scope-denied floor resend records already-known paid
usage as non-authoring discarded rounds; it cannot trigger another route or ACK.

Receipt ACK loss remains unknown: no persisted successful-request pending-ACK
outbox exists. No new paid request is purchased to reconcile a receipt. The exact
turn/attempt/task occurrence is recorded where available. Neither input confirmation,
consideration, task completion nor canonical delivery implies the other facts.

## Conversation execution identity boundary

Problem: carrying only a tenant and reconstructing every principal as a user can
silently turn a future system dispatch into an attended human executor.
Decision: `contracts/conversation_execution_identity_v1.json` defines a private,
server-stamped envelope in the existing accepted-attempt config. Public create,
resume and plan-execution config cannot supply identity, attendance or task
scopes. Authenticated subject/owner/tenant are retained; task scopes stay empty
rather than inheriting API scopes as durable tool grants. The executor validates
the envelope before context construction or registration, including recovery.
Present malformed, wrong-owner, wrong-tenant and nonhuman generic envelopes fail closed.
Alternative rejected: `_attended=False` alone is neither provenance nor a grant.

This remains **not automatic dispatch**. Generic system principals are rejected;
the separate private `_projectDispatchAdmission` carrier is admitted only through
validated internal `TrustedDispatch`, never public config or agent text.
Only absent legacy envelopes retain the existing explicit-owner user path;
explicit nonhuman/false-attendance markers cannot take that fallback. Existing
numeric-owner internal Goal continuations retain their existing semantics; this
boundary does not retroactively certify them as new project grants. No new loop,
thread or resident worker is added. Guard:
`tests/conversation/test_execution_identity.py`.

## Explicit-human bounded dispatch

The dispatch schema owns request, grant, assignment, admission, physical-attempt
ledger and review/view DTOs. An owning human with `chat` scope explicitly submits
a coordinator human turn and exact projection revision, distinct same-project
recipient, brief, model, token/request caps and Unix-second deadline. Admission
rechecks owner/tenant, canonical project, available coordinator binding, current
source revision/human provenance and grant revision/revocation. Synthetic input
is not authorization. The recipient is a system/chat principal, unattended and

brain-attributed; the ordinary user identity contract is not relaxed.

New grants pin the original positive coordinator binding revision. Every birth,
physical admission, dependency-evidence and publication gate checks that epoch;
A→B→A rebinding cannot restore old authority. Idempotent replay retains its old
pin. Legacy unpinned grants remain readable but cannot acquire fresh paid/file
admission or be upgraded in place; a new explicit human command is required.
Finite responsibilities also freeze their original binding epoch before triggers.

A scoped idempotent command maps to one assignment and the existing deterministic
conversation command, then an immutable turn/attempt association; task identity
and result status come from that attempt. Binding precedes executor birth.

Problem: the delegation binds (or, for a restart-paused continuation, advances)
the association only after its create/resume command returns. Under slow storage
the accepted-attempt recovery scan claimed such an attempt first and started it
unbound, so the executor refused it as not matching its "immutable birth
association"; the delegation's own claim then lost and the attempt stayed failed.
Decision: accepted-attempt recovery repeats the idempotent bind or exact
continuation from the accepted config before dispatch. A transient refusal
preserves the pending attempt; a definitive refusal settles it only while it is
still current, pending, unqueued and unbound. This fence is checked in the
terminal-event transaction. A timeout before that write commits leaves the same
attempt indexed for recovery; after commit, replay reads the settled attempt.
There is no start claim just to retire a refusal. A Stop or executor claim that
wins first remains authoritative. See
`test_project_bind_refusal_keeps_exact_attempt_recoverable`.

A successor reserved only by its own start claim
(`@dispatching:<attemptId>[:<owner>]`) or its exact durable-worker binding
(`conversation-attempt:<attemptId>`) is unbound evidence for association
continuation; any other binding still refuses. A refusal names the first missing
piece of evidence and its remedy: the predecessor attempt is not found, belongs
to another Turn, or is still running; the successor is not an attempt of the
continued Turn or is bound to another execution's task; or the pinned
association names another task
(`test_a_refused_continuation_names_the_missing_evidence_and_a_remedy`).
Alternatives rejected: a longer recovery grace (only moves the race), skipping
unbound dispatch attempts (strands them pending), and claiming solely to settle
a refusal (a timeout after the claim hides the pending attempt from recovery).
The claimed worker also binds through the shared dispatch-contention reread:
another ledger writer's commit is not an authority refusal. Guards:
`test_recovery_scan_between_birth_commit_and_bind_starts_under_the_grant_association`,
`test_continuation_accepts_a_successor_reserved_for_its_own_execution` and
`test_claimed_dispatch_binding_rereads_contention_before_executor_birth`.

### Fresh bind authority boundary

`repository.bind_execution` admits a registered, still-unstarted assignment
through the ordinary grant path, not a reconciliation authority. Its initial
validation checks exact owner/project/tenant/recipient/grant revision, the
original binding epoch, human source, durable Stop and dependency readiness.
The subsequent dispatch CAS checks the still-unbound association, grant
revocation and exact admission revision. An identical already-bound association
